THE DANGER SIGNAL.

A red flag is recognised as a danger signal on every road. But there are other danger signals,many of them not so apparent as a red flag, though they betoken worse dangers that are to be met on any road, even in these days of swift motors. When your palate, like the man with a red flag on a railroad, stands at the entrance to 'your throat (so to speak) and waves off every mouthful of food he sees coming down the hn, what does that portend? Why, danger—a very real present clinger, which must be got out of the way with as little delay as possible or disaster will result. It is now little more than six years since such a danger as this threatened Mrs. M. Frond, of 19. Edgar- street, Bromley-by-Bow, London, E.—had, in fact, been threatening her for a long time. In her letter, dated March 29th, 1904, Mrs. Frond says: "'Food used to cause me pain and tightness across the chest, and very often pain in the back, between the shoulders, as well. Wind was a constant trouble to me, and I was seldom free from headache. I had always a sickly feeling, and sometimes a cold fluid would rise into my mouth, causing a sinking sensation at the stomach that was -very distressing. These symptoms, together with fre- quent attacks of dizziness, proved that my complaint was indigestion and biliousness. At last my health became so bad that it was only with the utmost difficulty T could get through my housework. "From time to time I was attended by a doctor, but be did not succeed in giving me any permanent relief. 'Not until I began to take Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup did I find my condition improve. That wonder- ful medicine seemed to go to the very root of my trouble, and I was soon on the high road to recovery. 'One by one my troubles disappeared, and soon I was able to announce, with perfect truth, that Mother Seigel's Syrup had completely restored me to health. At this moment I am as well as ever I was in my life." Forcing food down—which is what some people do— is worse than useless. The best work is willing work. When Mother Seigel's Syrup has set the stomach right we don't have to force food down any more; the appetite craves it, the stomach is ready for it, and the danger signal is hauled down.

MANNERISMS OF GREAT CRICKETERS.

The habits and mannerisms of cricketers are often interesting, and sometimes not a little amusing (writes E. H. D. Sewell in" The King.") Many a bowler when hit for four,stands on one leg and looks at the spikes of his delivery boot, while the policeman is sending back the ball and losing his helmet in one and the same action. This artful attempt at blind is really so thin, it is very nearly time some bowler thought out some- thing elsc. When I first had the honour of playing with W. G. Grace, I could not help noticing how he. when telling his side the order of going in, invariably said, "Cane in third or seventh wicket down," or whatever was your place on the menu. Force of habit. All his life he had been in the habited seeing the rest of his side come to him at the wicket, so that he never like every captain in the world does, made use of the verb "to go." Another bowler I wot of when he is being punished goes to and quite scrupulously examines the ground where his delivery boot comes down, and then, looking at the sole of his boot, picks off every- thing round the spikes down to a blade of grass, and also in an affectionate sort of way taps the ground with his foot where he comes down as he delivers the ball before he goes hack to deliver the ball. Anthony of Notts strikes all sorts of quaint attitudesafter delivery, whether the ball has beaten the bat or not; while who has forgotten the "My word! "attitude of poor merry little Johnny Briggs as he saw a ball fizz past the bat and just miss the ash wood?

===== 0002601_19040709_art0010.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN'S CHAT.

Artist and Teacher.—Mr. G. F. Watts, R.A., will long be held in affectionate regard for the way in which he has helped us by his pictures to realise that death is not anevent which we ought to regard with dread whether for ourselves or for others. This is a mstter in which I most of us have something to learn from Cicero, whom we are accustomed to regard as a pagan. He was con- sinced that death was the gate of life, the beginning of that which is alone worthy of the name of life, and Mr. Watts has assisted us to feel that Cicero was right. There is no King of Terrors in his paintings which present to us death as the merciful and benignant friend. His grave and earnest purpose is indieated in "Love Triumphant" where the victory over Time and Death is portrayed, and in many other works he places both Love and Death on a level very different from that of the majority of the old masters. Among other services to his country he has presented to the nation a repre- sentative selection from his best works. As he himself said, his intention was not so much to paint pictures that charm the eye, as to suggest "great thoughts that will appeal to the imagination and the heart, and kindle all that is best and noblest in humanity." I Mr. Watts on Drink and Gambling.—The eminent I painter seldom missed an opportunity to condemn the Igambling spirit which has taken such a great hold upon Society. Betting and drinking he said would lead to national degeneration, and these vices were even more in evidence in the upper than in the middle and lower classes. No one ever disgusted Mr. Watts more than the young girl who talked to him at dinner of her suc- cesses in playing Bridge, and of the money she had won at cards. To him, the desire to enrich oneself at the , cost of another's loss, was dispicable, in the extreme, and he never hesitated to express his strong opinion upon what he regarded as the two great evils of our time,—gambling and drinking. Eastwell Park.—Eastwell, near Ashford in Kent, where the King spent the week-end on a visit to Lady Gerard, is where the Duke and Duchess of Edinburgh passed the early years of their married life. Their eldest daughter, now the Crown Princess of Roumania, was born there, and Princess Beatrice, the youngest of their children was also born there in 1884. The Duke never cared much for Eastwell, as he was of a decidedly economic turn of mind, and found the place too large and expensive to keep up. It now belongs to Lady Gerard, the widow of the second Baron Gerard, and her son, the present peer, will come of age a few months hence. Society Scribes.—Lady. Marjorie Gordon whose marriage takes place on the 12th of this month is one of tbe most accomplished women writers in Society. When still only a child, she edited that bright little paper for children called "Wee Willie Winkie, and her clever I play entitled "The Fortunes of Nigel" was very favour- ably received. Amongst other well-known women who are contributors to literature are Lady Clementine Waring, who also entered upon editorial duties whilst still a school-girl, Lady Warwick, and the Duchess of Sutherland. Lady Sarah Wilson it will be remembered I acted as war correspondent in South Africa, and Lady I Colin Campbell, Lady Jenne and Lady Violet Greville, all rank as leading lady journalists. Holidays on the Motor Car.—Such is the affection which some people have developed for the motor car that one sometimes hears them say that they never go by train now. The last time they did so, they will go on to tell you, was on such and such occasion, months ago. It is amongst this class of people that we may , expect to see the motor car much in vogue this summer, I for camping out tours. Many are arranging to spend their holidays in this way, taking a light camping out- ' ftt,and mapping out their tours independent of hotels and regular stopping places. For such a purpose the car has obvious advantages over the old caravan, which was altogether too slow for the majority of people. The motor is more self contained, whilst it can easily • travel 50 or a hundred miles a day thus enabling the I party to spend their time in constantly varying sur- ronndings. Lord and Lady Dudley.—Lord Dudley has been a motorist for many years, but the most experienced ill the management of cars.sometimes meet with accidents, , and His lordship appears to have had a narrow escape from injury the other day. He was ascending a steep hill when the gear gave way, and the car commenced to run backwards. This is even a worse predicament than a runaway car, and Lord Dudley could only turn it into I the bank with the certainty that it would be violently bverturned, though fortunately this happened without • causing serious injury to the !occupants, Lady Dudley is also a keen motorist. She was a Miss Gurney,coming from the well-known family of Quakers, and bankers, and became the adopted child of the Duke and Duchess of Bedford. Her wedding made a great sensation and Lady Dudley is still one of the beauties, as well as one of the most charming hostesses in society. Lifting the Summer Frock.—The short skirt of serge or tweed is the choice of a few independent spirits. but the exquisite linens, muslins and taffetas, are the vogue, for summer gowns. All these of course have the trail- ing skirt, and the brief and partial reign of the gown that clears the pavement seems to have made us more awkward than ever in the general mode of carrying the I dress. This is an art in which the Frenchwoman excels, I but which we seem unable to emulate, and one sees many distressing and inelegant illustrations of the fact in the parks and fashionable resorts. One woman will clutch as much of her dress as she can, and bunch it up in a heap in front, letting her underskirt attend to itself. Another will boldly hold up both garments on one side, and display an undu/amoimt of hose. Still more inele- gant is the method of holding up the dress on both sides with the hands resting on the hips. This is known as the market woman style, and gives a comical appearance even to the most stylish attire. The best way of carry- ing the summer frock—audit depends a great deal upon this for its success—is to lift it neatly on one side, free of the underskirt, and rest the hand there without any pronounced appearance of leaning on the hip. Tan Shoes for the Holidays.—For summer and seaside wear, tan shoes have carried all before their. At one time it seemed not unlikely that white canvas footgear would be the fashion, but this generally leaves an in- complete aspect to one's attire, and fortunately it has I not come, into favour. Tan shoes are also greatly affected by American women, both in their own country ! and by those on a visit here, and they generally take :.care that the colouring of the dress and shoes shall be somewhat in harmony. The fashionable Parisienne attaches still more importance to matching the colour of her toilette and footwear, but we give less attention to this matter, and as tan shoes are all the rage, the majority of girls wear them, independently of any such considerations. Belts.—New and stylish belts are every day making • their appearance in the shops. There are kid,swede and Ileather varieties, also the shaped pieces of gold and silver elastic, plain and embroidered. The waistband of the moment is well boned, and fastens high up the back, with quaint rosettes. There is a deep point in front which is also ornamented with rosettes, the centres of them being not infrequently filled with paste buttons. MADGE.

A WATERY GRAVE FOR DOGS.

At Stockport on Wednesday, George Sanderson, an insurance agent. of Ann-street, South Reddish, was fined is, and costs for drowning a dog in the Ashton Canal. Last week no fewer than 83 carcases were taken out of the water, and numerous complaints had been made of the stench.

FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

Our English climate is so exceedingly variable that it necessitates a greater number of garments being neatly packed away in our travelling trunks when we set forth as so many do at this season of the year to seek health and recreation at the seaside. One has to be prepared for all and every kind of weather from the scorching mid-summer's day which makes the lightest and finest of muslin gowns the only wearable garb to a chilly, bleak, or wet and cold afternoon when one revels in the cosiness of a warm serge or cloth costume and meditates seriously on the neces- sity of a fur necklet when out of doors. Hats and boots to meet the requirements of both exigencies must naturally be included in our outfit, while an intermediate costume of linen or crash preferably is a sine qua non. There are, however, such pretty ready-made skirts with material and trimming to be turned into a dainty bodice or a smart bolero that the last mentioned style of dress need not be viewed as a very serious or expensive item. A. girl of to-day, however, will find herself reAy in need

of a dress of this kind and can pick up some charming varieties at annual summer sales. I think, indeed, that these sales are a godsend to most girls whose dress allowance is not over large, for the last few years have seen the best of the summer after July. A pretty, simple linen dress is shown in my sketch. It is of pale sea-green linen with the yoke spotted with pale blue embroidered dots. The mode is a plain one, and the charming little toilette would be smart enough for town as well as country wear. Buff coloured linen is embroidered in white and pale brown and looks very smart worn with one of the soft kid belts studded with gold that are so fashion- able. A gold elastic belt embroidered with clear pale sea-green stone worn with a gown of this style excited my admiration as it clung so closely to the slender waist of the wearer, and the dull sheen of the gold looked well against the soft toned linen. Ido not fancy either that it was so expensive as the kid belt I previously referred to. Belts and buttons are quite a feature of smart toilettes this season, but both can be rather costly if one follows the leaders of fashion. The care- ful-minded can, however, choose the happy medium and regret alike the ultra costly and the rubbish. A set of pretty buttons for a blouse or dress makes an exceedingly accept-

able gift this summer, and these can by bought in a pretty little velvet lined leather case which shows them off to advantage and gives the recipient some idea of how they will look against the background, of a similarly tinted blouse.

A charming blouse is given in my second sketch, the front of which would show off a set of dull gold buttons set with coloured stones to advantage. The blouse is of soft, thick , white silk with a yoke of piece lace of a pretty pattern and good quality. The sleeves are made in a fanciful fashion with a double pouf ending in a soft frill. The mode is a charming one and not likely to be imitated in the ready- made blouses. I always think that, however really one appreciates the advantages of the ready-made garment, one always prefers that its history remains in oblivion and hopes that the beholder thereof will imagine it is in reality made to order. Why this should be so it is impossible to say, for the fact that our clothes are ready-made does not mean they are worth- less or cheap. Still, womankind arb decidedly conservative in their leanings and dislike the idea that the make and mode of their toilettes is common_ property to be purchased at a minute's notice by one's next door neighbour should she feel so inclined.

ER HAT.

Millinery is, of course, much in one's mind in summer, and the very charming summer hat depicted in my last sketch shows the latest mode in this year's hats. It is particu- larly distinguished by the high jam-pot crown trimmed at one side by a cluster of pink and red roses, while a soft lace scarf is twisted carelessly round it and loosely knotted at the back, falling thence on to the shoulders. A stiff wreath .of roses is arranged across the front under the large up-turned brim. The hat in this instance is expressed in fine straw, but I have seen some very pretty models of the same shape made entirely of lace ar.'d trimmed solely by flowers. The high crown puts it out of the question for a tall woman to wear a hat of this mode as it would seriously increase her apparent height and will, there- fore, be welcomed by all those of short or medium stature. The hat with the low flat crown has become so generally worn that some ch,ange is naturally welcomed as a relief to the monotony. Flowers this year are very generally worn, and a pretty hat I recently admired was trimmed with hydrangeas and soft, dark violet colour velvet which har- monised with the flowers.

For a simple gown of pale blue serge, suitable for yqiChting and seaside wear, the skirt and coat are ornamented with small tucks and narrow rows of fancy braid, and the revers are faced with white silk. • The blouse is of white chiffon, with fall kilted I frills hanging out of the sleeves, and the hat is of pale blue chip trimmed with soft folds of white chiffon spotted with black. A plain parasol of white silk, edged with a. band of pale blue, is carried with this smart little costume, COQUETTE.

The Examiner Saturday, July 9 1904• STORY OF 1 SONG. What I am about to relate occurred a number of &years ago, a short time after that popular song, "He Turned :Her Picture Toward the Wall," came out. I was then living at Branton, out in Western America, our family consisting of myself, my wife and two children, Mattie, aged . fifteen, and George, aged seventeen. We possessed an organ upon which my daughter played, George singing, Mrs Wilters and myself coming in in the chorus in regular country fashion. I was the musical enthusiast of the party, and while I did not like all the songs then extant, when one-did strike me I immediately mastered it. I went into ecstasies over this particular song, and whistled it in the wood- shed, hummed it in the parlour, sang it to visitors, neighbours, and friends. Many of these took the fever but mine was especially malignant, and the song haunted me for weeks after everybody else had caught on to something new. My wife cas-aally mentioned an asylum for lunatics several times a day. But I still repeated the song, and the first thing on rising in the morning, and the last thing on retiring at night. Then she expostulated, and ventured to hope that no more popular songs would come out for at least a year. I realised that my state was becoming alarm- ing. Something must be done, and immediately. "Mrs. Wilters," I said, "the thing shall be stopped." "But how, dear?" she queried, wearily. "I shall lock up the organ." Which I did; but the song still ran in my head. At last I collapsed. I was ill from an overdose of music. The doctor said I would recuperate after,a few days of rest, but upon my becoming convalescent I must refrain from all music. Right in the midst of this mental tribula- tion something happened. Mrs. Wilters staggered into my bedroom, one morning, her eyes as large as saucers, and exclaimed : " Sam Wilters, every picture in the parlour is turned to the wall !" "Yes," chimed in George, "and the organ is unlocked ! " "And pa," added Mattie, "the organ stops are open where you play 'He Turned Her Pict ure.' " Instead of throwing me into a mental fever, this information did the reverse, it broke up the musical trend of ray thoughts. "Reverse every picture," I commanded. "Lock the organ and fetch the key to me." I was obeyed. Then I said— " George, go and fetch my gun from the attic." "Oh!" gasped My wife. "Yes, I will sit here in bed, armed, and at the first approach of danger leap from it and shoot the person who is perpetrating this joke." "But you are too ill to think of anything of the kind," expostulated my wife. "I shall need only to step from the bed and fire. Pity if I cannot sing a popular song! We'll see about it!" "It will be murder," said Mattie, with tearful eyes. "It won't be murder! It'll simply be defence against a burglar. See?" They all saw; and as I was master of tilt. house and thoroughly aroused to the situa- tion, it was decided that I should go on picket duty that night. "Mrs. Wilters, you will retire to the chamber above," I ordered at bedtime. "And Mattie, you can accompany your mother. As for you, George, get a cudgel from the wood- shed, and become a sort of body-guard to me." As night set in the darkness in the rooms became intense. Not a flicker of light any- where; just total darkness. George sat in the parlour behind a case of books containing poetry, prose, and enough dictionaries to scare an ordinary burglar out of his wits. .I sat bolt upright in bed, my back resting against the headboard, my trusty gun in my hands. The clock struck, one, two, three. The sound of the bell had scarcely ceased when a loud noise came to my ears. Striking a match, I peeped into the room where George was sitting. He was fast asleep, and his falling cudgel had awakened me. I lighted the lamp and stared at the pictures. "George!" I shouted. "What, father?" he cried, starting from his chair. "See!" He staggered back. All the pictures were again turned toward the wall, the organ was unlocked, and my favourite stops were out!" George stared at me. I stared at George. "What does it mean, dad?" "You slept!" - "But you were on guard?" "Yes." "And did you sleep?" "I think not." "How came.you here then?" "The falling of your cudgel aroused me." "From sleep." "Perhaps." Mattie and her mother soon appeared on the scene, but none of us could offer a solu- tion to the mystery. Daylight came. The organ stops were replaced, the organ relocked, and every picture righted. The next night Mrs. Wilters and myself were to go on guard, she to remain in the parlour, as George had done the night previously, and I in bed, as I was not quite strong enough to remain up. At midnight the house was again quiet, Mrs. Wilters on guard. I heard the clik strike twelve, then one, and— My hair stood on end. A scream came from the parlour. Hastily lighting a lamp, I beheld Mrs. Wilters standing in a corner, swinging a stick frantically and screaming. "Eleanor, what in Heaven's name are you doing?" • "You nearly frightened me to death!" she gasped. "I?" - "What did you fire at?" "I had discharged the gun, and it lay smok- ing against the footboard, the looking-glass having a round hole through it. "Did I fire?" "Did you? Mr. Wilters, to-morrow night Mattie shall be on guard, and neither you nor your gun can frighten her!" "Great Heaven, see!" Every picture was again turned toward the wall, and the organ was open! "Eleanor, what mystery is this?" My wife was speechless. Just at this moment George and Mattie appeared. "Father, we will try it to-morrow night, and failing, we'll call in the police to watch for us," George said, when the situation had been explained. And so once more the pictures were turned back. "I prefer a revolver to a cudgel," Mattie said stoutly. Thus armed we again awaited events. Singular that I should hear the clock striking every night toward morning! But so it was. It struck twelve, then one, then two, and— I leaped from bed. Mettle was firing her revolver rapidly, the light revealing her in an attitude of .despair. "Well, Mattie, what have you hit?" "Nothing!" she said doggedly, throwing her smoking revolver into a corner. "Oh, yes, you have You- ve shot four holes through my new picture." As we came back to a normal condition of mind we found that all the pictures had been reversed as before. "It is terrible !" said Mattie. "But why did you shoot?" "I heard footsteps." "Whose?" "I do not know." "Leave me. to watch to-morrow night," said my wife, determinedly. "Well and good r we will !" I answered. When the fated hour came Mrs. Wilters commanded— "Mr. Wilters, yen will now retire as usual." I retired gun in hand. "George—Mattie—your father has gone to bed. We will retire. Come!' The three left the room, going to the spare chambers above. What it meant I had no means of knowing at the time, but it all came out afterward. When I , fell asleep they returned to the sitting room, each holding a revolver and a dark lantern. Mrs. Wilters was at the head of the undertalting, her idea being to flashst bu the ll' usn uo ipsoen. tahne dparloursti ouki

a person DC Cil•••;t: • •••; •-•LI —,••••ttwer. organ or the pictures, to shoot him. As usual I heard the clock strike twelve, One, two, and The next I knew the parlour was suddenly illuminated, and crack!, crack! crack! went the three revolvers. They had surprised the man in the very act of displacing the pictures, and after firing excitedly, rushed into the room. -"Great heaven!" cried my wife, fainting and falling. "Are you hurt?" gasped George. "We have shot father!" screamed Mattie, springing forward and clasping -me in her arms. Matters were soon righted. But if my bold warriors had not been too badly frightened to shoot straight, I should not be telling this story. It is all explained by the fact that I wae a somnambulist, and did these things in my sleep."

THE PSYCHIC NAME.

By the time Freddie Lawrence has made another million or two it would not be sur- prising if he endowed a school for the ad- vancement of clairvoyance. Yet six months ago you couldn't have found a greater scoffer at all forms of mysticism. The change came about in this way. Six years ago Freddie was one of some forty young men who wanted to marry a cer- tain young woman. All of Freddie's rivals seemed to have hopeless advantages inherited or acquired over him, and Freddie grew so dejected that he became even less attractive than usual. Then the young woman married him, though Freddie has been unable wholly to believe it ever since. Freddie's wife came to dance and talk and laugh with other men and accept Freddie with resignation. These other men were clever, most of them, or otherwise favoured of fortune like the thirty-nine rivals who had once driven Freddie to the brink of despair. Freddie would have thought sooner of sui- cide than of finding fault with her. The only wonder he had was that she hadn't found him out before. He lost weight rapidly, smoked black cigars to excess, spent thirteen hours a day at his desk, and had protracted fits of abstraction when time was worth very much money to him.

Also he struggled for a while against fate. The constancy and delicacy of his attentions might have won a princess. Then he complained to her reminiscently, and she did not understand and was annoyed. Then lie tried simple dignity. Then mute pathos. At the end he was seeing her auout twice a week at dinner.

One afternoon Freddie slammed his desk to and left his office in the middle of the afternoon because be felt if he didn't go out he would have apoplexy. He tramped about the streets for miles, hearing nothing and see- ing nothing and nobody until he ran into or over them.

For a man whom nobody had ever accused Of being sentimental or imaginative, Freddie was thinking queer thoughts.. He was won- dering if he should do some heroic deed—save a little child or an old man or half a dozen people—and was mangled or crushed (fatally perhaps) in the operation, whether when he was brought home by an admiring policeman and an ambulance surgeon his wife would brush back his hair from his forehead as she used to do. The picture was so pathetic that Freddie almost wept. - He might have wept bad his eye not been arrested at that moment by a remarkable sign on the ground floor of a house which he was passing. This was the sign The Only Greatest Veiled Lady Isis. Psychic, Palmist, Clairvoyant, . _ _ _

Past and Future Fully Revealed.- The Sepa- rated United. Lost Affections Restored. Valuable Advice on Love, Marriage, Health, Sickness, Divorce, Law- suits, Speculation, Business. Nothing Too Difficult. Freddie read the sign over two or three times. Then he picked out the clauses about the " separated" and the" lost affections" and dwelt on them. Then he looked up and down the street hastily, plunged into the corridor of the house and pulled the bell at the Veiled Lady's door.

A young woman with her hair in curl papers ushered him into a half darkened front room hung with Oriental looking and not particu- larly clean draperies. There was a stuffed baby alligator on a centre table, with some- thing burning in a bowl beside it which smelled bad, and various other outlandish but inexpensive bric-a-brac. "Now don't say a word," said the Veiled Lady as she appeared from behind a velours portiere. "You're in love, poor fellow, and .the lady is cold. Now, ain't I right?" Freddie started. There seemed to be sOme- thing in psychics. His funeral face relaxed a trifle.

The Veiled Lady was a jovial, fat little prophetess, and she seemed to like Freddie's looks. She took off her veil after the first formal moment and even offered to send for wine if Freddie‘wished it, and would provide the wherewithal. But Freddie didn't want any wine. He wanted clairvoyance. "Well, then, you tell me the whole story, dear," said the Veiled Lady, "and I'll go into a psychic state and fix ye up in a minute. You're a married man, ain't you?" Freddie started again. The Veiled Lady certainly was a wonder. He told her his story, omitting names and points of identifica- tion, of course, and the• most harrowing de- tails. It seemed more tragic and hopeless to him as he told it. But the prophetess surveyed Freddie'S personable figure and her black eyes flashed like matches. "I don't have to go into a psychic state for such an easy case," she said. You've just got to tell me one thing—the lady's first name." The lady's first name was Mildred. ' said the veiled lady, "just as I supposed. The whole trouble comes from your not calling your lady by her psychic name. She was named Mildred, you know, by mortals. Now her phychic name is—er— Flossie, and when you call her anything else you jar the astral influences—disarrange the auspicious conjunctions, you know. Isn't that plain? " Freddie regarded the phophetess with s strong and sarcastic eye. "Now, when you go home," she went on, "you watch your chance and call her Flossie. Not often—say once every day or two. And be sure and do it absent-minded like—as if you was thinking of something else, you know —because that gives the influences—well, you do it and see." _

--Freddie felt only a strong desire to get out in the fresh air and objurgate himself. "How much do I owe you?" he asked. "That'll be ten pounds," said the veiled lady, suavely. " Ten pounds!" echoed Freddie. "For the foolery you've been talking. Your card says half a guinea a reception, and that's a great deal too much." "That's all right about my card, dear," said the veiled lady. "This is different." She stopped and regarded Freddie searchingly. "You don't think my reading is worth ten pounds?" she asked. "I don't think it's worth a single shilling," replied Freddie. "Well, I'll tell you what we'll do then," , said the prophetess. "Don't pay me a single shilling now. You go home and try my scheme. Then come around next week and pay me just what you think it's worth. Is that fair? " Freddie didn't like the plan. He wanted to be rid of the veiled lady. He offered to compro mise for half a sovereign, then for a sovereign. He would have paid almost all she asked, finally, but the veiled lady pushed him gently to the door and out. She seemed to like the proposition the more she thought of it. "That's all right," she said. "You como round next week. But be sure and do it absent-minded, darling." Then she shut her door and Freddie made r sroresrel.e h e knew ft— " What the ehe was siel xse so wholly Nnvl 1:011i/I ys. for the open air. fd that in sheer despera- FrHedisdiewhifaednd'tintellie raetmhoFotoremnesdte intention of mak- , p unresponsiveit ng iroenscah ei pf ioaodl n ,soafabi nudidti " What have you been doing to-day, Flossie, dear?" "Why 'Mildred' I suppose, what else?" asked Freddie, lying without a quiver. Then in two dozen words she told him of her do- jugs that ART.

Two nighis later Freddie was dozing eti the library couch when his wife came home from a dinner. She passed by him and he put out his hand and seized her sleeve. "Hello, Flossie, dear," said Freddie, sleepily. • Freddie is a heavy man when he is sleeps; but she had him by the shoulders sitting up- right in less time than it takes to tell it. Freddie—Freddie Lawrence," she de- manded, her eyes and cheeks blazing and looking far more lovely than Freddie had ever seen her, "what have you been calling me? What do you mean by calling me Flossie? Who is Flossie, Freddie, dear ? "

Freddie lost his mind temporarily, but his soul expanded like a balloon, and the rest is sacred history. • The next afternoon Freddie was ushered into the Veiled Lady's sanctum.

"Well, birdie," ajsked that genial prophet- ess, "do I get my ten pounds? "You do;" said Freddie, handing her a note. "I might have sent it to you, but I wanted to come "—Freddie showed emotion—" and tell you that you're .all right."

The Veiled-Lady looked' at the banknote as a matter of business. It was a twenty pound one.

"You're all right, too," said the phophetess. "But, say, dear, don't you ever try that psychic name again. It wouldn't work."

WARRINGTON DRAUGHTS LEAGUE.

Annual Report.

The following is the annual report of the Warrington Draughts League. " The Warrington Draughts League was formed in 1897, for the purpose of promoting and encouraging the study and practice of the game of draughts, and consisted of the following clubs : Warrington,Cairo-street, Penketh, Padgate, Stockton Heath, and St. Barnabas. Mr. R. A. INaylor, Cuerden Hall, Thelwall, kindly accepted the , office of • president, to which he has been annually re- elected. Mr. Frank Dunne was elected chairman of Committee, with Mr. D. Plinston as secretary, and both have been annually re-elected. Mr. E. W. Clarke, Penketh was the first treasurer and ratained the office for several years. Mr. A. Perris, vice-president, presented a hand- some draught board and set of men to match,as a trophy for the first season's competition, and, after a contest carried on throughout with great zest, it was finally won by the Warrington Club, Cairo-street being a close second. The next season, 1898-9, Mr. W. Chalmers, one of the vice-presidents, presente I the League with a very handsome silver cup as a championship trophy, which has since been known as the" Chalmers" Cup. It was put 'up for competition conditionally that it became the property of the club winning it three years In succes- sion. The same clubs entered as in the previous year, and the competition aroused great enthusiasm among the players, and was very keen, finally resulting in victory for Cairo-street, with Warrington a close second, thus reversing their respective positions of the previous year. Cairo-street also won the next season's competi- tion, 1899-1900, Warrington again being second. The season folio wing, Warrington succeeded in carrying off the trophy, Cairo-street being second; and it was evident that none of the other clubs had much chance against either of them, and consequently interest in the competition was somewhat weakened, and one or two of the clubs dropped out. In 1901-2 Warrington were again victori- ous, and finally in 1902-3 won the cup for the third time in succession, and it passed finally into their keep- ing, in accordance with the conditions of the competi- tion. The League was now (1903-4) without a trophy, but the president, Mr. Naylor, generously came tl the rescue, and promised to provide a new one, and oppor- tunity was taken by the committee to extend the sphere of the League, by inviting works teams to enter. For the purpose of placing the clubs upon a more equal footing as regards playing strength, a list of some twenty of the best players was made out, and submitted to the committee, and a rule was passed that no club should include in any League team more than two players whose names are on the list. This important alteration of the rules infused new life into the League, several works teams joined, the composition of the League being as follows :—Warring- ton, Cairo-street, Padgate, Richmonds, Monks Hall S-. Co., Fletcher Russell and Co., and Crosfields. The new competition has aroused great interest in the town, and bids fair to widely extend the usefulness of the League. The playing strength of the clubs under the new conditions was at first an unknown quantity, but after half the season's fixtures had been completed it became evident , that the ultimate result lay betwoen Cairo-street and Richmond's and after a remarkably keen and interesting competition, Cairo-street came out the victors, and are again first holders of the new championship cup; Richmond's came out a close second. The new cup will be presented to the winners by Mr. Naylor at a meeting to be held at Cairo-street schools, the date of which is not yet fixed. Prizes will also be presented to each member of this winning team. The following table shows the complete scores : LEAGUE TABLE.-1903-4. Matches. Games. P. W. L. D. W. L. D. Pts. Cairo-street l2 12 0 0 ... 113 28 29 ... 21 Richmond's l2 9 2 1 ... 84 50 30 ... 19 Fletcher's l2 6 6 0 ... 66 82 28 ... 12 Warrington Padgate l2 4 7 1 ... 61 83 34 ... 9 Monks Hall l2 2 8 2 ... 62 82 26 ... 6 Crosfields l2 111 0 ... 43 110 20 ... 2 It may be of interest to note that in this season's competition each club played two matches with every other club, making a total of 42 matches played, in which a grand total of 594 games was contested. These consisted of 492 wins and 102 draws. The greater the percentage of draws the better the skill displayed; showing that in this respect there is plenty of room for improvement. In addition to the league fixtures, friendly matches were played with sevez al neighbouring towns. War- rington defeated Runcorn both at home and away ; won from Widnes away, but lost the return match at home, through having to play a weak team. Warrington also visited St. Helens and lost a close match by only one game. The return match has not yet been played. Since the formation of the League the standard of play has improved all round, and there is no doubt that future seasons will bring about a still further advancement.

- The Committee hope next season to enter a Warn i ng- ton team in the Lancashire County competition for the "Love " trophy, which carries with it the club's cham- pionship of Lancashire (this year won by Burnley), and trust that all lovers of the game will rally round and assist the League in its efforts to extend the popularity of the game, which is admittedly a highly intellectual recreation, free from gambling, and well worthy the support of the thinking public. IF DUNNE, Chairman. Signed -; D. PLINSTON, Secretary. W. WARBURTON, Treasurer.

UNDER DISCUSSION AGAIN.

CURE OF RHEUMATISM BY DODD'S KIDNEY PILLS BRINGS UP A FAVORITE TOPIC.

Rheumatism and its cure is always an interesting itudy. and in this connection a story comes from Nor- folk that is well worthy of consideration. It tells of the complete cure of a bad case of Rheumatism by Dodd's Kidney Pills. The story as told by the patient, 'Mr. W. G. Davey, Old Tower, Barnham, Thetford, is as follows : _ _

"I was a martyr to Rheumatislr? caused by working in a river bed, and by further work in damp surroundings I aggravated the Rheumatic pains which struck me across the loins. This worked up into my shoulders and arms and the pains were so severe I could not rest at night. My limbs were swollen at the joints, my fingers were all drawn up in the palms of my hands and I was perfectly helpless. As I was only twenty-one my outlook was extremely ill. "I was advised to try Dodd's Kidney Pills which I did, and got relief from the first dose, and after taking four boxes was able to go to work. I rapidly put on flesh and have not had the least sign of Rhennuktism

A TRAGEDY OF' UNREQUITED LOVE. An inquest was held at Leominster on Thursday, on Arthur Matthew, footman who shot in the back a fellow-servant named Nary Sargent, with whom he was in love, and then fatally shot himself. The'girl Sargent has now recovered from her injuries. From the evi- dence it appeared that the recent 'death of Matthew's mother and his unrequited love preyed on his mind to such an extent that he refused food, and said he was going to die.

PLAGUE OF BEES AT PENDLETON. 1 A swarm of thousands of bees buzzed for the greater part of a day round a Pendleton lamp-post. Scores were killed by children,and many wandered into houses in the vicinity.

EVENTS OF THE WEEK.

THE WAR IN THE FAR EAST.

At the time of writing the situation in the .Far East appears to have suddenly contra- 'dieted itself in the most remarkable manner. Last week the Japanese after a hard fight of some days took the Motienling Pass and the 'Taling Pass, which have, as the key positions to •Liaoyang, been 'their objective • for some time. And directly afterwards 4 'Comes the news ...that they have alien back again 'before General, 'Count Keller ' and evacuated ‘i 11114.11, 11( these great strategic points. • 'C 0 n fi rmalion, . however, and an explanation .\\ •• A I is being awaited: 41. , And, in addi- f!j111 I tinn, comes the announcement of the beginning 'of the torrential luly rains which 'turn Manchuria into a series of rushing torrents and render comparative small streams impassable.- This makes it appear that Liaoyang must be taken at once or not for some time, and this makes the Evacuation of the passes, if true, all the More difficult to understand. In the mean- while the Japanese are reported to be within the outer ports of Port Arthur and gradually drawing nearer their goal. Marquis Oyama, ' the new Japanese Commander-in-Chief, who is 1,0 take supreme charge of the operations in alanchuria, has left Japan and will direct all 'the armies in their great eornbined strategic iiievement, for which they have been pre- Paring ever since the outbreak of hostilities. At sea the Russians, though held in check by Admiral Togo, seem not only to have the Passage into Port Arthur comparatively free, but also to have got a far _larger proportion of their original fleet than was expected into fighting order. But Admiral Karnimara, on "the other hand, is reported to have got into touch with Admiral Skrydloff off the islands south of Korea after several weeks of watch- ing and waiting. But everything must be taken with a grain of salt, as most of these rumours come via St. Petersburg, whilst the Japanese cables are conspicuously silent, a fact which so far has .as a rule proved nnlinous for Russia.

LIVELY SCENES IN PABLULMENT.ths

Last week, after a curious period of apathy, the House• of Commons seemed suddenly to become galvanised into a state of excitement and action. For some weeks past the Opposition, mainly under the leadership of Mr. Asquith, with Mr. Winston Churchill

fighting in stren- uous guerilla .14k,a fashion and " infecting his un- official neigh- bours, has been attacking the Government with more vigour than previously, hiL.l and, in contrast, „,)! 1. , the Unionist .41‘) members divi- dedamongst 1)" themselves, / seemed daily to grow more 7 apathetic, which may be ascribed to natural reaction MR. T. after a long period in the majority, and the fact that a hundred or so do not intend to offer themselves for re- election, being tired of Parliamentary life. As the Liberals grew more determined, fighting with the old Parliamentary weapon of obstruction' the position of the Govern- ment, deserted by its friends, grew daily less secure, till the Premier found himself forced to take the drastic step of threatening to resign unless supported by a proper majority. Last week his followers woke up and accord- ed him the support he demanded, but with it increased the obstructionist tactics of the Opposition. The Licensing Bill was making no headway, and the Aliens Bill was being blocked word by word and phrase by phrase in committee, so Mr. Balfour announced his intention of applying the "guillotine," which means closuring a Bill by compart- ments. The result was that last Friday there took place one of the most heated and tumultuous sittings the House of Commons has seen for years, and many violent speeches were. made. Amongst them not the least so was Mr. T. P. 'Whitaker's onslaught on the Premier, accusing him of flinging knighthoods to his Manchester supporters and keeping the Cabinet under the control of the publicans. Mr. Whitaker, who is one. of the leading advocates of the Temperance movement, is Liberal member for the Spen Valley Division of Yorkshire, and has sat for the constituency since 1892. It looks as. though lively times were ahead before the prorogation, or possibly, the dissolution of Parliament.

THE 'VARSITY CRICKET MATCH

The Cambridge team at Lords were un- fortunate in not being able to win the match against Oxford, as after outplaying their opponents at all points time stepped in and robbed t,em of what could be safely said an easy victory. SANTOS DUMONT AND HIS AIRSHIP.

The greatest sensation of the St.lLouis Exposition has been the injury to M. Santos Dumont's great balloon, which was waiting to make its first ascent on July 4th. It was found in its shed all hacked and muti- lated with the silken gas-bag cut beyond possibility of repair, and, as the particular material is only

obtainable in France, the as- cent was put out of the ques- tion. It is a

curious coinci-

dence that the

airship

M. Sintos Du-

mont brought

over to the Cry- sal Palace in

1.902 was simi-

firly mutilated

j usf before 'the

ascent, and the

mystery

, V A V M. SANTOS DUMONT.

===== 0002601_19040709_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
never been pro- perly explained.

The

detectives

in charge could find no clue and ultimately ascribed it to careless packing. At St. Louis, however, Colonel Kingsbury, chief of the World's Fair Guards, after investigation has officially re- ported as his opinion that M. Santos Dumont had himself ' had his airship mutilated to avoid making the ascent, a theory which has caused enormous sensation. M. Santos Du- mont denies the charge, which amounts to one of cowardice, with the utmost indigna- tion, classing it as absurd and outrageous. As he points out, he has taken every risk in connection with aerostatics for years without flinching and has devoted his life and his iortune to it, so that he is not likely to cry off at the last moment. The charge, though seriously made, certainly looks 'preposterous on the face of it. The Exposition authorities have offered a reward of a thousand dollars for the discovery of the guilty person. A man by the name of Charles Meyer was arrested but released again. He strongly denied any knowledge or complicity. Will the mystery be cleared up this time, or will it remain unexplained as at the Crystal Palace two years ago? _

ROYAL HENLEY WEEK.

The Most Famous of Regattas.

Not to know Henley argues one's self unknown in the Rowing world. No aquatic event since Noah set sail in the Ark interests so many people as the great July week at Henley. The famous Regatta Course is lined with Houseboats visited by all that are famous and fashion- able. The races themselves attract oarsmen from all the world, from Yale to Parramatta.

===== 0002601_19040709_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
RACE BOATS AND HOUSE BOATS,

A racing boat, with its thin mahogany planks, its wide outriggers and sliding seats, designed to give the long, long pull which spells victory in a race, are verit- able works of art. So, in their way, are the Houseboats which wealthy families use as Summer Homes, resting here week, there a month or more, on the Banks of the Thames. And most of all just now they congregate at Henley.

_

A Henley _House Boat,

The fact that the eyes of all England are on Henley this week gives special interest to the words of a Henley man, Mr. Charles Sarney, in a local paper. Four years ago, when he was lying helpless in Reading Hospital with paralysis and spinal disease, Mr. Sarney thought he would never be able to handle oar or scull again. For the hospital authorities told him he was incurable, and sent for his friends to take him away. "But," said Mr. Sarney (whose homel is at Royal Oak Cottage,Gray's-road, Henley,) "I read in a newspaper of a man, afflicted like myself, who had been completely restored to health by Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people." "Why don't you try them ?" asked a friend. Mr. Sarney did try them, and their effect was extra- ordinary. Before he had finished one box he could actually walk round the table with the aid of a stick. This was after months and months in bed. After he 'had got partly through the second box he went for a short walk ! His friends were amazed to see him out, and asked him what he had taken to bring about such a marvellous recovery. He told them, and gladly, that it was owing to Dr. Williams' pink pills that he Wft§ able to get out of doors again. After the third box he was able to throw away his stick. He persevered with the pills and when he had taken eight boxes he was look- ing for work, after nineteen months without doing a single stroke. This is a remarkable cure, but there is no difficulty in explaining it. Paralysis is a disease of the nervous system. It is a sign that the nerves are ill-nourished. The food of the nerves is blood. Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people make new blood. Ordinary medi- cine could not cure Mr. Sarney. Reading Hospital was perfectly right in sending him away as incurable by ordinary medicine. Dr. Williams' pink pills have cured thousands of persons whom ordinary medicine could not benefit, because ordinary medicine does not make new blood. Any ailment that arises from lack of blood, or from poisoned, impure blood, as anxtnia, skin diseases eczema, chronic erysipelas, rheumatism, gout, can be speedily cured by Dr. Williams' pink pills; and dis- orders arising indirectly from lack of blood,as neuralgia, spinal disease, indigestion and bile (which are caused by the blood not being able to absorb food from the stomach) can be cured in the same way. But it mast be the genuine Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people that are taken—not a substitute. Genuine pills bear the full name. Dr. Williams' medicine !company, Holborn- viaduct, London, will send a box post free for two and ninepence (or six for thirteen and nine) : but they can be had at all m3dicine shops if you insist on the genuine. •

Parish Church Choir Trip

The adult members of the Parish Church choir had their annual excursion last week end, and a most en- joyable affair it proved. Leaving Warrington at 1.15 on Friday afternoon the"party arrived at Bettws-y-Coed the delightful North Wales resort at 4.30. After tea at the Gwydyr Hotel, visits were paid to various in- teresting places. The party slept the night at the hotel and after breakfast left Bettws at nine for Blaneau Festiniog, where they joined the Narrow Gauge Rail- way and travelled through the beautiful Vale of Fest- iniog to Portmadoc. From this place a drive was taken by coach to -Beddgelert, passing the pass of Aberglaslyn. After luncheon at the Prince Llewellyn Hotel, the journey by coach was resumed through the Vale of Gwynant and the magnificent scenery excited general admiration. After a short stop at Penygwryd, the drive was continued through the pass of Llanberis, Llanheris being. reached about 4.30. After tea at the Padarn krms Hotel, the excursionists left for Warrington, which was reached at eleven.

AGAIN, AND AGAIN, AND AGAIN.

Again we ask you to drink Vl:Cocoa.

WARRINGTON TOWN COUNCIL.

THE SMOKE NUISANCE. Can it be Abated ?

THE APPLETON WATER SUPPLY.

There was not much important business at the meeting of the Warrington Town Council on Tuesday evening, and the proceedings were unusually brief. The Mayor (Alderman Bolton) presided and there were also pre- sent: Alderman Fairclough (deputy mayor), Alderman Sutton, Francomb, Hutchinson, William Pierpoint, Bennett, Smethurst, Councillors John Evans, Tinnion, Burton, P. Peacock, A. Peacock,Ellison,James Evans, T. Barton, W. Brough, Lord, Pemberton, Horrocks, John Roberts, Forshaw, H. Thornton, T. H. Ashcroft, D. Duncan, Stott, Rogers,Hulse, S. Davies, Maginn, Wilson, with Messrs. Lyon •Whittle (town clerk), S. Wilson (deputy town clerk), J. Fairhurst (borough treasurer), T. Longdin (borough surveyor), Dr. Gornall (medical officer), W. S. Haddock (gas engineer), J. Deas (water engineer), J. Moore Murray (director of education), E. M. Ker (deputy surveyor.)

===== 0002601_19040709_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LATE ALDERMAN HEATON.

A letter was. read from Miss Heaton, on behalf of her mother and herself, expressing heartfelt thanks for the kind vote of condolence, passed at the last meeting, on the occasion of Ow death of Alderman Heaton. COUNCILLOR JOHN EVANS ELECTED AN ALDERMAN.

The first, business was the election of an alderman to fill the vacancy occasioned by the death of Alderman Heaton. "

The voting papers having been examined, The Ma'ma said the voting was practically unanimous for Councillor John Evans, whom he declared duly elected an alderman of the borough. (Applause). One paper .was blank, and another contained the name of Councillor John Evans, but was unsigned. All the other papers recorded votes for Councillor Evans. Alderman EVANS thanked the Council for the honour they had done him, and said he appreciated it very much.

Alderman EVANS was also elected presiding alderman for Fairfield Ward. EDUCATIONAL BUSINESS.

" HEARTSEARCHINGS " ABM: A NEW GATE. Alderman BENNETT moved the confirmation of the minutes of the Education Committee. He alluded to the departure of Mr. Herbert Parkinson, who, he said, had been appointed to a position elsewhere, and the next in command would take his place. They congratu- lated Mr. Parkinson on his promotion. They were about to lose the services of Mr. Gow,who had obtained anappointment at a considerable increase of salary at Blackburn. They were sorry he was going, and hoped they would get another man equally capable to sucleed him. The alderman then alluded to the extra accom- modation to be provided at the Secondary school by means of the sub-division of rooms,. and the proposed appointment of two additional assistant masters and mistresses. They were sadly cramped for accommodation, and even when they had made the sub-division, the accommodation would not be equal to the demands upon it. That, however, was the best they could do for the time being. In order to provide proper tuition for the 60 extra students, they would be obliged to appoint two more teachers. Councillor ELLISON seconded.

Councillor THORNTON alluded to the appointment of a caretaker at the Heath Side school at a salary of £lO.l us. It seemed to be rather a heavy amount. Council- lor Thornton also drew attention to the new gate which had been ordered for the Education Offices in Sankey- street at a cost of £4 19s. They were cutting down all the expenses they possibly could, and he thought an ea:planation of this item was necessary. Alderman BENNETT said the salary was fixed accord- ing to scale, and it depended on the size of the school, and the accommodation. As to the new gate, he had had some heartsearchings, but the amount was small. and it would not be wise to say too ,much about the matter. He was not quite convinced as to the necessity of the gate, but to some extent the Education Com- mittee were not to blame, as the new gate had been obtained on the recommendation of the Borough Sur- veyor.

The MAYOR explained that the caretaker had to find all labour. The old gate was practically beyond repair. Councillor THORNTON : The gate is made, and yet we come here to state whether it shall be made. (Laughter.) Councillor A. PEACOCK said some members thought a cheaper gate would have been sufficient for the time being. Councillor THORNTON : I move that the minutes be passed with the exception of the one re the new gate. The MAYOR ruled Councillor Thornton out of order. Councillor Lord also rose. The MAYOR: You are too late. (Cries of Chair, chair)." Councillor LORD : I don't agree with that.

The 2,rA.YOR : You will know next time. You mast not be too late next time. (Laughter.) You mist move an.amendinent .straight away.

The minutes were then C-axried. - POLICE PROMOTIONS.

COUNCILLOR WILKINSON'S APPOINTMENT ON THE WATCH COMMITTEE.

At a meeting of the Watch Committee, on the 23rd June, the following resolutions were passed :—That Detective Barlow be promoted to the rank of Ist class sergeant with plain clothes allowance; that Detective Forster be promoted to the rank of 2nd class sergeant with plain clothes allowance; and that Sergeant Williamson and P. C. Clouch be allowed plain clothes allowance in lieu of uniform. It was resolved that Mr. Councillor Wilkinson bo appointed a member of this committee in the place of the late Mr. Alderman Heaton.

The NlA.vort moved the confirmation of the minutes. and said the police promotions would make very little difference as far as the expense to the Corporation was concerned. Alderman FRANCOMB seconded.

Alderman PIERPODU said he could not possibly have any objection to the appointment of Councillor tVilkin- son because he knew ho would make a most admirable and useful member of the committee. Not very long ago, the Council determined that a memb,r should not have seats on more than six committees except by special permission of the Council. Coun- cillor Wilkinson was already on seven committees. Was he prepared to resign his seat on one committee? Committees were inclined to make their proceedings in the nature of a family party. It was natural when there, was a vacancy for the members to say," kVe should like so and so," but that took the choice out of the hands of the Council as a whole. Some of the members had seats on only two or three committees, or, perhaps, four. He would take the' cases of Councillors Peter Peacock and Stott. men of evident ability and young councillors, and they were only on two committees. The MANOR replied that Councillor Wilkinson wished to be on the Watch Committee, and he was promised the next vacancy. He was a regular attender, level- headed, and Understood the business.- He had the first claim so far as the younger mom'-‘ers were concerned. When he (the Mayor) entered the Council he was kept for sevefal years on only one or two committees,.

Alderman' PIERPOINT- asked if .Councillor Wilkinson was prepared to resign his seat on one committee, and the Mayor replied in the affirmative.

The minutes were then carried. HEAVISIDE SCHOOL TRUSTEES AND rHE WIDENING OF GOLBORNE-STREET.

At a meeting of the Finance and Estate Committee held on the 13th June, it was resolved that the plot of land remaining after the widening of Golborne-street (the site of the property recently belonging to Mr. Greenough) be allowed to be used by the Trustees of Heath Side school for the purposes of a day school subject to their paying a proportionate part of the ground rent and undertaking to give up possession of the land in the event of the school bein,, transferred to ; the Council. The Town Clerk reported that Mr. Thomas Halton had commenced an action against the Corpor- ation for damages, and it was resolved that Mr. Whittle i defend the action.

THE STORAGE AT THE APPLETON RESERVOIR. "WATER RUNNING TO WASTE." At a meeting of the Water Committee, held on the 20th June, the question of increasing the storage at the Appleton reservoir by means of deepening the same was considered, and at the conclusion of the business the committee proceeded to Appleton to make an inspec- tion of the works.

Alderma,n FRANCOMB moved th 3 confirmation of the minutes.

Alderman EVANS seconded.

Councillor Tusio-N-T--a-sii-€4-for some information with reference to the Appleton reservoir. It was a very important matter, and he saw that the committee had inspected the reservoir.

The MA.yoR nothing. Councillor Tr.wroN : It is time they did something. Continuing, he said there was plenty of water there running to waste which could be utilized and soldat a profit. He had always thought the Water ComMittee did not take this matter in hand as they might have done,and had been frightenedat the seemingly consider- able expense and the imaginary danger with reference to the banks. He hoped they would take the matter into consideration, as it was most important that they should have water at Appleton to sell and not allow it to run to waste. He also hoped the committee would let the Council have a report upon it so that it might be considered, because, while he was not in favour of a £70,000 scheme, they might,- at reasonable cost, he thought, get a much larger simply of water than they had at present. -(Hear, hear.) In reply, Alderman FRA.NCOMB stated that while the committee were not prepared that night with a scheme, at the next Council meeting they would have a very full report ready. (Hear. hear.) The committee, as already said, had been to Appleton, but personally he did not think they were in a position to judge. They must have expert evidence on the matter. It would be a very difficult thing to clean out the reservoir, and it was in such a state that he thought expert opinion was necessary. With regard to the impounding of the water they might get a scheme to do that. (Hear, hear.) The minutes were then approved. ELECTRICITY AND TRASIWAY 'MATTERS. MECHANICAL V. HUMAN STOKERS. LAWN TENNIS" NEW BLOOD."

One prominent feature of the Ali-England Champion- ships this year was undoubtedly the arrival of what may be termed" new blood." If critics of the position of lawn tennis at the present time ever had any grounds for asserting that new talent was practically extinct, such has been removed by the capital exhibition given at Wimbledon last weak by the younger generation. It is true that lawn tennis demands a lengthy apprentice- ship and considerable physical capacity, but these qualifications do not necessarily mean the con- tinued supremacy of the "old gang," Even if the net result of the matches contested gave the balance of power to the men previously at the top of the tree, their triumph was only obtained at the expense of the greatestexertions and the defeat of some of their numbers. In the case of Ritchie's victory over Eaves, for example, we have an illustration of one generation succeeding another—a na:tural evolution which some people seem to imagine did not apply to lawn tennis. Greville's defeat at the hands of Riseley is another instance in point, as also the same player's easy triumph over H. S. Mahony, and his brilliant victory over Gore. In the case of ladies, this gradual "shouldering out" is even more proriounced. That plucky and honoured player, Mrs. Hillyard, who re- turned to the arena again last week, discovered that several younger exponents now exist who are more than her equal. In the case of Mrs. Sterry there must have been a some- what similar awakening, and it is not necessary to go further to show that a new force is in being. Whether the standard of the best ladies' play is higher to-day than it was when Miss Isottie Dod was in her zenith is another matter; but it is certain that the average standard of ladies' play, as witnessed at first-class tourna - meats to-day, has itnineisurably improved. If other proof were wanted, it is to be found in the fact that so many ladies are now handicapped on the same mark while the breach between those at the highest limit anti those who come next is so small as to be almost undis- cernible.—A. WaLLis MYERS, in "The King."

Fe;icitations to the A STRAIGHT ISSUE Government candidate IN OSWESTRY. for the Oswestry Divi-

i 0 n. Mr. OLIVE 'BRIDGMA.N is determined that there shall be no misunderstanding about his attitude towards the fiscal question. Elsewhere the unfortunate chain- Pions of the Ministry in the bye-elections have 'either been ingloriously mute on the subject, or have run away from their brave words. Mr. tRIDGMA.N, at all events, does not mean 'to follow their example. Mr. BRIDC, MAN declares himself . a strong supporter of the Sheffield policy, and also of preferential tariffs. Moreover, the Tariff Reformers, excluded from 'other contests, are going to fight this out boldly. They have been told by some Unionist candidates that their room is pleasanter than their company. But Mr. BRIDGMAN is a man of mettle. has raised the CHAMBERLAIN standar:l iu Oswestry, and the Tariti Reformers are flocking to it with Joyous energy. They have already settle down in the villages, we are told, to instruct tne natives lii the economies which are to raise the price of 'everything without making anything dearer. They tried this demonstration in Mid-DEvaii without Much success,and we do not suppose that the rural voters in Suropshire will be more easily converted Still, it is refreshing to nave a straight issue once More, and to find a Unionist candidate who does 14low what his colours are, and is not so ashamed of them that he tries to keep them out of sight. Mr. BRIDGMAN, at all events, does not shuffle between Sheffield and Birmingham. He knows they were the same thing, and is not afraid to say so- It is a pleasure to meet such a straightforward Candidate, and the gratification of beating him .vill he greatly enhanced by respect for his personal Character. Mr. BALFOUR has a chance now of finishing the Session with some remnant of his legislative pro- gramme. Tnere is one obstacle ahead. Toe new Liberal Unionist Association his to decide next Week whether it will formally include Mr. Cita.n- a3tRLAIN's tariti proposals in its official policy. IMechanical v. human stokers was another topic round which a desultory discussion waged. The !Electricity and Tramways Committee has decided !on the purchase of four mechanical stokers at a cost of nearly £l,OOO, and claims that a saving of ! .50 per cent. in the price of fuel will accordingly be " effected. It seems strange that there should be the difference of opinion there is in the engineering world with regard to the respective merits of the mechanical and human stoker. Some firms in Warrington have thrown the former on the scrap- heap and reverted to the latter, so that the experi- ment of the Electricity and Tramways Depart- ment will bo watched with interest. Even Alder- man Bennett—the man of poetry, figures, and dreams—had something to say about stok- ing, though he admitted his ignorance of the mechanical aspect of the question. His concern was—smoke. The human stoker he dubbed a failure, except as regarded his capacity for killing vegetation, dirtying curtains, and des- troying the " domestic felicity " in Alderman Bennett's little home. Therefore Mr. Bennett ' hoped much from the mechanical stoker. That ,he will keep a sharp eye on the tall !chimney at Howley no one need doubt.

It is to be hoped that the portly tom 9 which the Medical Offioer of Health for the Borough has issued this week will not be allowed to he on the tables of our local legislators,unread and unheeded. The report grows in bulk year by year. Two years ago it was compressed into 78 pages; last year it increased its size to 87 pages and this year there is a further advance towards portliness by the addition of 30 extra pages. These one hundred and odd pages have a forbidding look about them, filled as they are with statistical tables and details of the work of the Health department-, but there are many points which deserve, and ought to receive, serious con- sideration from those who have the welfare of the town at heart. Our average death- rate for the past ten years reaches the kivel of 19.7, which is not altogether satisfactory,althongh we may congratulate ourselves that it is below the average of many manufacturing towns. The dis- cussion which has recently taken place on the health of the town will have served a useful purpose if it has educated public opinion on the necessity of payinz more attention to those measures which contribute to the health of the community. There are many valuable suggestions in the report of the Medical Officer which will be found summarised in another column. The recommendations in regard to the schools are of great importance and in paying more attention to school hygiene than was possible under the old system, the Education Committee will be doing a real service to the community.

The word of warning which is convoyed in the Medical Officer's report to the Paving and Sewer- age Committee wider the head of unpaved streets and passages should be reiterated in the Council Chamber until active steps have been taken to remedy Vie existing state of affairs. A printed list is given, covering eight pages, of all the un- paved streets and passages in the Borough, and the undue length of this list only serves to prove how woefully behind we are in this question (if paving. Some of the streets and passages still unpaved were even mentioned by the Medical Officer of Health twenty years ago. Why have no steps been taken ere this? Coug- cillor Tinnion has just taken upon his shoulders the responsibility of Chairman of the Paving and Sewerage Committee, and he could not better sig- nalise his promotion to this onerous position than by initiating a forward policy in this matter of street plying. We hope we have heard the last of the ex.cuses which have been so often urged that it is impossible to get stone or the necessary material and labour. The ...latter is urgent if we are to have a healthy town and prevent the recurrence of epidemic sickness, which proves so distressing and costly. "This want of paving," says the Medical Officer, "is one of the most serious insani- tary conditions at present existing in the town." Nothing could be more clear and definite, and if the members of the Town Council shirk their duty upon them must be thrown the blame for any evil results which may follow their neglecc.

A statement made by Mr. J. W. Knowles at the Rural District Council meeting on Wednesday might, if allowed to pass unexplained, create a wrong impression. Millions of gallons of Corpor- ation water, it was stated, were being allowed to run to waste in a ditch near Croft. We have made inquiries into the matter, and find that the waste is quite unavoidable. , Shafts are being sunk at the new pumping station at Delph-lane, and unless water were pumped out, it would be an impossibility for the men to work. The quantity of water to be dealt with has proved considerably larger than was anticipated, and additional pumps have been requisitioned. It is hoped that the work will be completed in a month's time. At present only one of the Dalph- lane shafts is in use, and when the contract is finished the town will have a water supply suffi- cient to meet the needs of the population for many years to come.

The Sowerby division electors have pronounced judgment on the Protectionist movement to the same purport as the rest of the constituencies which have been polled since Mr. Chamberlain's ill-starred policy was launched. A majority of 1,410 in the Khaki Election has been increased to one of 2,170. Not only has the Liberal vote largely increased ; what is still more significant, the Unionist poll has suffered an appreciable fall- ing oft. No more striking proof could be afforded that the reflecting men in the party are holding aloof from candidates who give any support to the Protectionist crusade.

Exactly a year has passed since amid gea flourishing of trumpets the Tariff Reform League was called into existence. To celebrate the -occa- sion of the anniversary, the League has is3uoii a statement of its work during the twelve months. How many millions of leaflets it has issued, how many meetings it has hold, are all set forth with impressive eloquence. Unfortunately, on the very day that it appeared in the " Times " the effect was completely marred by an article in the leading Conservative organ, the "Standard," in which the League and its work were roundly declared to have been a ghastly failure so far as converting the electors is concerned. "The constituencies," we are told,"will not tolerate it,, nor does their dislike abate with fkimiliarity." If the collapse of the Unionist party is to be averted, says this faithful monitor 'there must be a plain and uncompro- mising disavowal of a policy which has done nothing but harm to the Unionist cause."

Not long ago the Tariff Reform League pub lished a statement in which the distress in Lancashire caused by the cotton shortage in tile United States was held up for the contemplation of Mr. Winston Churchill. No one but the most ill-informed or unfair of controversialists would have suggested that there is any connection what- ever between the weather in the Southern States of the Union and the Fiscal Ques- tion. However, as the Tariff Reform League was responsible for the insinuation, it can only be supposed that that organization is gravelled for lack of more reputable arguments. The point is only of importance since it enables Free Traders to point out that Great Britain is, as a matter of fact, by no means the worst sufferer from the cotton shortage. At the recent conference at Zurich it was conclusively shewn that, of all the nine cotton-manufacturing countries represented there, France had experienced most acutely the effects of the cotton crisis. And one has yet to learn that France is fanatically devoted to Free Trade.

The Earth Tremor.

The earth tremor which seems to have been pretty general in the Midlands and Peak district, was slightly felt in Warrington on Sunday afternoon. Mr. Charles White says that at 3.15 in his house in Sankey-street the furniture was !lightly moved for .a few seconds. Several other inhabitants also testify to the same effeet.

LOCAL NEWS.

Coming Events. The annual sermons will be preached at the Baptist church, Latchford, to-morrow (Sunday) by the llev. J. G. Greenhough, LX., ex-president of the Baptist Union and the National Free Church Council. Mr. C. E Parker will address a young people's service in the morning.

The Rev. John Yonge will preach at Wycliffe Church to-morrow (Sunday). In the morning his subject will be "The seed is the Word of God," and in the evening he will speak on "Heroes of shipwreck and heroes of social wreck."

An open meeting of the Warrington P.S.A. Society will be held in the Co-operative Hall, to-morrow (Sunday) afternoon. The Rev. H. Minnie° will be the speaker. There will be a great attraction on the Wilderspool Football Ground on Saturday next, in the annual tournament promoted by the National Fire Brigades Union (North Western District.) An exceptionally interesting programme has been arranged, to conclude with the "house on Fire " scene. -

The Warrington and District Agricultural Society's annual show will be held at Walton Park on Thurs- day, August 11th. Prizes, cups and medals to the value of £750 are offered.

The Widening of Market Gate. The Corporation has decided to proceed forthwith with the demolition of property at Market Gate, and notice to quit has been served upon several occupiers of shop property there.

Passive Resisters' Sale at Lymm. The goods of the four Lymm passive resisters, Mr. G.. L. Welford, solicitor, Manchester, Rev. S. R. Woodall, Primitive Methodist minister, and Messrs. Henry Champ and W. Taylor Moss, were sold on Wednesday afternoon on the steps of the Lymm Council offices. There was a fair attendance. Mr. W. Hickman, auctioneer, Warrington, officiated. The goods, which included a copper tea and coffee urn, lady's gold chain, lady's gold brooch, and a silver presentation urn, were all bought by one person for the exact amount of the rate. Afterwards speeches were made by Mr. Henry Champ and the Rev. S. R. Woodall, condemning the Education Act.

Mr. Birtles's New Photographic Studio. The new studio which has just been erected to the order of Mr. Birtles, the well-known photographer, to meet the requirements of a growing business, is an altogether up-to-date and well appointed building, and one which will therefore give increased facilities for the work of artistic portraiture, while adding greatly to the comfort of Mr. Birtles's sitters and other customers. Entrance to the new building—of which, by the by, little is to be seen from the street—is gained through the entrance hall of the present studio at Legh House. The new studio is a spacious room, some 44 feet long by 16 feet wide, with excellently arranged lightings enabling the most subtle •' effects" to be secured, the furniture and other accessories being also of an artistic yet home-like charalter as distinguished frcvn much of the theatrical stuff in use by many professional photographers. Under the studio are equally extensive workrooms in which the various processes of developing, printing, fixing, toning and mounting can be conveniently and expedi- tiously carried on. There are also facilities at hand for out-door work when dealing with large groups and for the photographing of hors-es and other animals. Mr. Birtles is to be congratulated upon the comple- tion of the new premises and in thus adding to the business attractions of Warrington. In the use and enjoyment of the new studio we wish him a full measure of the support of his townsmen. The Local War Memorial.

A meeting of the Workingmen's Committee, which is raising funds for the local war memorial, was held on Thursday evening, Mr. D. Davies presiding.—The hon. secretary (Mr. C. J. Newton) read several letters he had received since the last meeting. Mr. H. Ashton (secretary of the Warrington Football Club) wrote stating that his committee had decided to subscribe three guineas, towards the war memorial.—The Chief Constable,in acknowledging receipt of a letter asking the Police Athletic Club to assist the meinlrial fund, said he would lay the matter before the Management Committee. He added that he thoroughly r -cognised the worthiness of the cause.—The Longford Wire Co.,wrote that the month's collections amongst the workmen had realised id 12s. 4d.—The Chairman said the com- mittee's communication to the Cycling Club would be considered at an early date and he had no doubt something would be done.—Mr: Hope (the hon. treasurer) reported that the amount standing to the credit of the committee .at the bank was £:74 Ss. 9d. There was a further sum of at least 25 or £3O in the hands of the collectors at the various works.—A. discussion ensued as to the advisability of holding a concert in the autumn at which a competition would be held for school children. It was proposed to offer prizes for the best recitation of Alderman Bennett's stirring poem "The Song of the Forgotten Dead."— It was decided to write to the head teachers to see if it was practicable for squads from the schools to compete.
