The Examiner, Saturday, July 9, 1904

Personal. Captain IL A. Denham (of the depot of Orford), the King's Liverpool Regiment, has been selected for the adjutancy, which recently became vacant, of the 4th battalion (militia). The battalion trains at Altcar during August.

Among the names of those from the Liverpool University upon whom degrees were conferred at the St. George's Hall, Liverpool, appears that of Mr. Richard H. Albery, the physics master at the War- rington Secondary School and Technical Institute. Mr.Albery graduated as Bachelor of Science in 1901, and took honours in physics in 1902. He has now obtained the degree of Master of Science. The Rev. A. F. White, who for the last seven years has been in charge of St. George's Mission, is leaving Warrington for Woolton, where he will have a curacy Tinder Canoi. Spooner, "His departure " says "The Parish Church Magazine," "will be a great loss. The work he has done is in all respects excellent and the results in the numbers of the congregation, of the com- municants, of the Sunday school, of the day .school, and other institutions connected with the Mission are most satisfa e+ory."

At Knutsford, on Wednesday, Arthur Robinson, motor driver, Northwich, was fined £5 and costs for driving at a speed dangerous to the public, on Sunday, 12th ult. It was stated that the defendant, who was accompanied by Dr. Alarkel, of Warrington, drove full twenty miles an hours. Dr. Markel said they were trying to overtake another car.

UNDER the CLOCK for GOOD Boom.—Advt. Band Success.

_ We are pleased to record the success of one of our local bands, at a contest held in aid of the local hospital at Verdin Public Park, Northwich, on Satur- day last, the Warrington League of the Cross Band being awarded the second prize in the selection, the test piece being "Recollections of Flowtow." They also secured the first prize in the quick step, the test piece of which was "The Southern Cross." Great credit is due to their conductor (.11r. T. Hynes) for this double success, and it is to be hoped they will not rest at this, but strive to .bring many more prizes to Warrington.

Picnic. The Firth Glee Party had a most enjoyable day on Saturday last, leaving Warrington at 8.30 a.m., for Matlock, which place they arrived at at 11.10 a.m. The various caves, caverns, and other places of interest were visited, after which the party drove to Ambergate, where a substantial tea was provided by Mr. S. Green, White House Hotel. After tea the members took the opportimity of presenting, Mr. E. Roberts with a beautiful meerchaum pipe and tobacco pouch for the past energetic service which he has rendered to the party.—Mr. Roberts suitably acknow- ledged the gift by a well thought speech.—During the stay at Ambergate several glees were sung to the delight of a large crowd that had assembled.

PUBLIC BENEFIT, UNDER the CLOCK for BEST BOOT sin LANCASHIRE.—Advt. Death and Funeral of Mr. W. Atherton._ _

Many Warringtonians will regret to hear of the death, after a brief illness, of Mr. William Atherton, at his residence, 201, Padgate-lane. The deceased for a number of years carried* on business in Gaskell- street as a provision dealer, and also held an off- licence, but a few years ago •retired from business. He was 75 years of age, and leaves a married daughter. The funeral took place at Padgate, the last rites being performed by the Rev.G.Vale 0 wen(curate-in-charge of Orford). Amongst those present in the church and at the graveside were Mr. John H. Draper (son-in-law), Mr. W. Atherton (grandson), Mrs. Shaw (sister), Mr. and Mrs. Southern (brother-in-law and sister), Mr. Nixon, of Birmingham (brother-in-law), Mr. Thomas S. Steel (solicitor), Mr. W. Habberley, and others. There was a number of beautiful wreaths and floral tributes.

H. M. ASHTON'S LIFE Tone is the KING OE' TONICS.-63, SANKEY-STREET, Warrington.—Advt. The Manchester and Liverpool District Bank. An ordinary meeting of the shareholders of the Manchester and Liverpool District Banking Co., Ltd., is to be held next Friday at the head office of the company, Spring Gardens, Manchester. The Directors report that the gross profit for the half-year ending 30th June last, after providing for all bad and doubt- ful debts, is £227,431 2s. 2d., to which has to be added the amount brought forward from 31st Decem- ber last, viz, : £34,040 168. us., making a total of £261,471 19s. id. From this sum the following de- ductions have to be made : Total expenses at the Head Office, London Office, and branches, including salaries, stationery, taxes, &c., £80,769 18s. 9d., half- year's provision for income tax, £5,000, leaving a net profit of £175,702 Os. 4d. The directors recommend th e declaration of a dividend of 21s. per share, which will take £131,250, leavinp• a balance of £41,452 Os. 4d. Of this it is proposed to carry to the reserve fund £lO,OOO and to next account £31,452 Os. 4d. The reserve fund, with the above addition, will then amount to £1,795,000.

Marriage of Mr. George F. F. White. The.marriage took place in Christ Chnrch,Patricroft on Wednesday morning of Mr. George Frederj.ck Free- and White, clerk to the justices of Warrington, son of Mr. H. Brown White, solicitor, of 18, Museum-street and Miss Dora Bowden, daughter of Mr. and Mrs William Bowden, Gorsefield, Patricroft. The cere- mony was very quiet, no invited guests being present The Rev. W. W. D. Frith, M.A., vicar of Christ Church, Patricroft, was the officiating clergyman. The bride who was given away by her father wore a travelling dress of pale grey canvas on silk, trimmed with white cloth and embroidered medallions, and a toque in silk chenille and tulle to match. The bride's only sister, Miss Ethel May Bowden, acted as brides- maid and was gowned in biscuit coloured voile on silk and wore a long gold neck chain the gift of the bridegroom. The bridegroom was supported by the bride's brother, Dr. Bowden, of Warrington. After lunching. at Gorsefteld( the happy pair left for Scot- land, where the honeymoon is to be spent. They were the recipients of nearly VA) presents. These included a solid silver tea and coffee service from the Warring- ton justices; a fish service and an illuniinatea address, conveying expressions of goodwill from the Warrington Borough Police Force and also a portable reading table clock from the Warrington press.

The Manchester Ship Canal Bill.

The petitions presented by the London and North Western Railway Company and the Warrington Cor- poration against this bill have been withdrawn, and it now proceeds as an unopposed measure.

The Vacancy in Howley Ward. The election of Councillor John Evans to an alder- manic seat causes a vacancy in Howley Ward. It a meeting of the Non-trading Committee,held on Thurs- day, it was decided to bring forward Mr. Benson as the non-trading candidate. It will be remembered that he was defeated last November by Councillor John Evans. The Conservatives have not yet selected a candidate.

Volunteer Man muvres. This (Saturday) afternoon the Ist Volunteer Battalion Prince of Wales's Volunteers (South Lancashire Regi- ment) will parade at 3 45, for a short route march to Moore, and will there be exercised in battalion drill and manceuvres. The mounted company, cyclists, machine gun section, signallers, stretcher bearers, and regimental transport wilt take part in the parade. Refreshments will be served on the field.

Accident at the Central Station. John Bostock, a single man residing at Stretton, met with a serious accident at the Central Station on Walking Day. Bostock is a shunter, and at 9.15 p.m. he was standing on the line when he was.knocked down by a local train from Warrington to Manchester. One of his legs was cut off and the other was broken, the limb having subsequently to be amputated at the Infirmary. Bostock is still detained in the institution and is doing as well as can be expected.

LADIES BEST •GLA.CE BOOTS and SHOES, UNDER the CLOCK. BRID GE-STREET.—A&. Sunday School Teachers' Trip. The tea,chors at St. Paul's Sunday School had their annual trip on Monday. The party, numbering 53,in- eluding the Vicar and Mrs. Bracecamp, left Warring- ton,at 12 noon, and drove through the beautiful Dela- mere Forest to Beeston Castle. On arriving at Bees- ton tea was provided. Then the party visited the castle and caves, and roamed about the grounds until p.m., when a start was made for home. Warrington was reached at 11.30 after an enjoyable outing.

Child Scalded to Death. Mr. S. Brighouse held an inquest at the Police Sta- tion on Thursday, touching the death of Noel John Buckle, aged six. months, son of Mary Constance, 80, Winwick street, Warrington, who died on Monday night, from scalding iniurie3.—lt appears that on June 22nd the mother put the child on the hearthrug whilst she placed a frying pan on the fire. She moved a pan full of boiling water,which tilted,and the contents fell on the child, scalding it severely.—A. verdict of "Accidental Death " was returned. Musical Successes.

A Conscientious Objector.

At the Borough Police C nut yestesday, Thomas Taker, Lovely-lane, applied for a certificate exempting his child from vaccination—Before the application was heard, the Hayor (who had been presiding), Alderman Smethurst. and Councillor Tinnion left the Bench, the Ilayor remarking "If any of you want to take vaccination cases, you can do so. "—Apolieant stated that he did not believe in vaccination. He had had four children, all of whom were born healthy, but after they had been vaccinated, he had "no end of trouble " with them. Three of the children had died. —Mr..C. S. Burgess intimated that he did not wish to grant the certificate andleft the Ihnch —The applica- tion was granted by Councillors Thornton, Wilkin-son. and John Roberts.

Parker very kindly acted as host and hostess to the bazaar executive and stall-holders. The attend- ance was encouragingly large, and Mr. and Mrs. Parker were assiduous in attending to the comfort and enjoyment of their guests. Crosfield and Miss Crosfield, and Mr. and 'Mrs. Armitage were among those present.

British Women's Temperance Association.

In connection with the above society a rummage sale was held in the Ragged Scho )1, which had been kindly lent for the purpose. Mrs. Garnett opened the sale at 3 o'clock, when the purchasers eagerly sur- rounded the stalls to buy what articles of clothing, they mostly, stood in need of, for with the majority money waS very scarce. Dress skirts were mostly in demand, several poor women being very much -dis- appointed at not being able to get one. If only. the hats could have been transformed into dresses, all would have been supplied.—lt 7 o'clock a very enjoyable concert c)mmenced, presided over by Miss Bessie Dutton. Hrs.- Jackson Holmes and her musical friends, along with other young ladies of the town,. gave great pleasure by their songs and violin solos The recitations from Miss Minnie Whitmore and Mr. Whitfield were very much appreciated. Mrs. Roberts, Sunny- side, sent ss. The proceeds, being a little over will go to the building in Manchester of the head offices for the Lancashire and Cheshire Band of Hope Union. Sergeant Barlow Complimented.

Sergeant Barlow, of the Warrington roree, was warmly congratulated upon his recent promotion at the close of an inquest held at the Police Station on Thursday evening.—Mr. S. Brighouse, County Coroner, said that before discharging the jury he would like to mention that Mr. Barlow had been lately appointed first-class staff sergeant. He would not be doing his duty to Mr. Barlow if he did not publicly recognize the services he had rendered to the jury and himself (Mr. Brighouse) during the time he had been Coroner's officer. Mr. Barlow was appointed to the post in September, 1898, and since then the duty haft been imposed upon him of report- ing 630 cases of deaths to the Coroner, and arranging for 309 inquests.. He (Hr. Brighouse) was not accus- tomed to flatter people, and he always said what he thought. He had no hesitation in saying that Mr. Barlow was one of the best Coroner's officers in the whole of South-West Lancashire. He knew exactly what he (Mr. Brighouse) required, and he reported the circumstances in connection with deaths in an intelligent fashion, thus enabling him (Ur. Brighouse) to decide whether, it was necessary to hold inquests or not. He (the Coroner) felt certain that Mr. Barlow made the way of both Coroner and jury pleasant, and—what was of far greater importance he made the ways of those who had suffered pleasant also.--.llr. F. J. Whittle, on behalf of the jury, supplemented the Coroner's remarks. He was sure Mr. Barlow was a very good officer, and well merited the promotion he had obtained.—Mr. Barlow said he could hardly find words in which to return thanks, but he would say that he had always tried to mix dis- cretion with law in discharging his duties.

PLAINTIFF'S HUSBAND ON-THE JURY.

At the Lewes Assizes last week a prisoner was charged with an offence against a woman. and after the trial had proceeded some time, it was discovered that the woman's husband was on the jury. A serious accident happened to Mr. George Done, of Gorton, near Manchester, on Sunday night, while cyc- ling from St. Helens to Gorton. About 10.30 he was riding down the brow near the Dam at Lymm when he collided with a Mr. Booth. Mr. Dane who sustained a fractured skull, was conveyed in an unconscious con- dition by the Warrington Police Horse Ambulance to the Infirmary where he was detained as an inpatient. Mr. Booth, who got a rather rough shaking, was able to re- turn home. Mr. Joseph Chamberlain celebrated his 68th birthday yesterday, having been born on July 8, 1836. PICTURESQUE PWLLHELI INVADED BY WARRINGTONIANS.

INCIDENTS OF A DAY TRIP.

The invading force was five hundred strong. From I nearly all parts of Warrington and a few outlying districts people had come to swell the ranks. Bank Quay Station was the place of assembly, the time, 5.30 a.m., on Saturday. The plan of campaign was an invasion of Pwllheli, but lest 'my readers imagine that there were warlike intentions, I may at once say that the mission was in the truest sense a peaceful one. Pleasure seeking was its only object, and in the attainment of that end Warringtonians are pre- pared to go a long way. It matters little to the day tripper that his repose must be cut short soon after daybreak, and that oftener than not, midnight will be passed 'ere he returns to his cot. You meet him hurrying to the station in the small hours, his face one vast, substantial smile, and when night comes although he may be thoroughly tired out, he has the proud consciousness of having achieved something—of having, perhaps, done a much harder though infinitely more congenial day's work than his employers will require of him during the week. Before Saturday the Warrington day tripper had probably never penetrated to remote Pwllheli. Whether he had ever attempted to arm-mance the name is still more doubtful. Mr. R. A. Naylor deter- mined that his employes at Howley timber works should have an opportunity of seeing the glories of the little Carnarvonshire resort, and the trip, was the outcome of his generosity. Employes, wives, sweethearts and friends made ito merry party and as usual Mr. L. Henshall, the genial manager, was at the helni. He takes a thorough interest in arranging for these outings, and it is gratifying to him when everything passes off well. By the way, the budding hopeful with matrimonial aspirations received every encouragement on trip day. Each employe over eighteen was invited to apply for a free extra ticket for his own particular lady friend," and it was plainly intimated that no sister or casual friend was expected to avail herself of this privilege. I have no doubt that lfr. Naylor's thought- fulness was appreciated in the proper quarter. There is the usual bustle when the train steams in, and at ten minutes to six we are speeding on our way. How refreshing is the cool morning air! There has been rain over night, and now the sun's rays light up the landscape, and all nature 'seems to rejoice. The travellers inhale some- thing of the freshness of this bright July morning, and everyone is in the happiest of moods. Past the hills of Frodsham and Helsby we speed, and soon quaint old Chester, with its placid river and weather beaten walls, is reached. Then on to Rhyl. Here we leave the beaten track,- as it were, and find ourselves amidst what are truly "fresh fields and pastures new." It is not long before we get a glimpse of the sea, and we looked out to admire Colwyn Bay and its fine promenade. Inland we feast the eyes on delight- ful mountain scenery. The richly wooded slopes,bathed in the pure sunlight, make such a 'charming picture that the trippers would fain indulge in an invigorating ramble. Perched on a hill-side, and einbowered in spreading trees, is seen Madame Patti's residence— Craig-y-Don. Picturesque Conway, with its grim fortress, carries the imagination to the "good old days "of feuds and warfare. The Morfa.—so familiar to many of our citizen soldiers—is dotted with tents, but although the trippers raise arousing cheer, most of the red-coats are. are constrained to silence, for they are on parade. Now the train winds in and out among the mountains We notice with some uneasiness that many of the peaks are capped with inky clouds. "If only they should burst!" we reflect. Presently a grand view of the Menai Straits is unfolded. Anglesey, with its rich woodlands, looks a real "gem of the sea," and we are revelling in this charming prospect when all eyes are directed on the graceful suspension bridge. A few minutes later the fine tubttlar bridge comes in for its due share of admiration. Bangor and its Cathedral are left behind, and at half past eight the engine steams into ye ancient borough ,of Carnarvon. Several passengers, eager to scan the news of the day, make their way to the bookstall, but I they are disappointed. The gentleman in charge assures them that, while the morning papers have not yet arrived, he has an excellent stock of picture post- cards! On leaving Carnarvon station we catch a glimpse of the old castle, standing sentinel as it were I over the town. Another run through beautiful mountain scenery brings us to the little junction of , Mon Wen. Here we join the Cambrian Company's single line, and in a few minutes—Pwllheli! This is evidently a great day in the out-of-the-world corner, for people are seen running excitedly down the hill side to watch the long train's arrival. The inspiriting strains of a band are heard and we—at least some of us—step from . the train with the air of conquering heroes. It is not so easy to pre- serve a dignified demeanour, when one has to leap a yard or two from a carriage to the ground. A. few excursionists who had to per- form this feat inquired loudly " Where's the landing stage ?" But Pwllheli is apparently not used to big day trips, and the platform is constructed accord- ingly." The band leads us from the ,station, and our triumphal entry to Pwllheli creates quite a stir, the inhabitants gathering. at street corners to gaze upon this "passing show." Our way takes es through the old-world part of the town. Facing the harbour, where several small ' craft float lazily, are trim white-wasited home- steads, had nicely laid-out gardens. Lovely fuschias ' flourishing in the open air remind us of Mona's Isle. Having traversed a few quaint narrow streets—with which Wa.rringtonians are not altogether unfamiliar-- we gain the fashionable West-end and a glorious sea , view bursts upon us. Now we can see what Pwllheli is really like! All clouds have rolled away, the sun beams radiantly from an azure sky flecked with the lightest of clondlets. The. magnificent sweeping bay, the dancing blue waters streaked here and there with a deeper hue, the background of rugged moun- tain scenery—peak after peak rising on all sides— make up a glowing picture which a painter might delight to depict. And such an exhilarating breeze ! It fills one 'with new life, and blows the cobwebs away—in a twinkling almost. Mr. Naylor and his esteemed sister are waiting to receive their guests, and there are pleasant smiles of recognition and cordial hand-shaking. Pwllheli is one of the well- known Carnarvon Boroughs and, as all my readere know, Mr. Naylor is assiduously wooing the constitu- ency. The place has a special interest for. us on that account. There is something fascinating, too, about the pronunciation of the name. When you look at the spelling you are inclined to give it up in disgust. Ask a native to enlighten you, and you are astonished to learn that such a mixture of letters can produce .a sound like " Poolthely." Only, .of course, there is an inimitable• Welsh accent before which the French nasal sound pales into insignificance. Breakfast is awaiting us at the West End, and with appetites whetted by the long journey the visitors need no pressing. The meal should have been served in a marquee on the Recreation Ground,but the wind willed otherwise. During the night the canvas was blown to the ground, and the tram shed had to be com- mandeered as a haven of refuge. It is a huge. family party when all the guests are seated, and the band dis- coursing at the entrance adds enlivenment—if any were needed.

! When one's time is limited, there is nothing like systematic pleasure seeking. Each tripper had been furnished with a handy little guide, so that no places of interest within easy access could be missed. Breakfast over, I join a small party intent upon exploring the Gimlet Rock which juts out abruptly at the entrance of the harlymr. The walk along the beach, strewn with innumerable pebbles, is delightful. How peaceful everything is ! None of the boisterousness of crowded, modern resorts has intruded here ! A short climb, and we are on the lower part of the Gimlet Rock. The ceaseless beating of the sea has hewn it: into a hundred fantastic shapes. As we stand—truly alone with nature —waves rapidly break upon the rock, sending showers of spray high into the air. One could dally here for hours, but time and excursion trains wait for no man,and there is so much to be done. We clamber to the summit of this won- derful rock, and the view 'repays us a hundred fold. Before is the broad expanse of open sea ; the moun- tain ranges stand out boldly across the bay ; whilst almost at our feet, but just a little in the background, the old harbour completes the pictur- esque scene. Retracing our steps to the West End we decide upon a tram ride to Llanbedrog, Mr. Naylor having generously provided the tickets. The metals are laid along the beach, so that we enjoy the full sea breeze all the way. The cars are one horse vehicles of a rather primitive kind. For a time all goes smoothly, but when the points have to be negotiated there is a jolt—the car is off the lines ! The little

Welsh conductor is heard talkirag rapidly n his mother tongue, and an unkind passenger invites him to relieve his feelings in English. Warringtonians have a way of rising to an emergency. The men soon put their shoulders to the car, and 'after a long, strong push the metals are regained. This is the only untoward incident, and we extract plenty of amusement from it. A short ride brings us to Llanbedrog—a charming rustic spot with a pretty, secluded bay. We lose no time in making our way to the Glen-y-Weddw grounds, the tickets presented by Mr. Naylor again being the "Open Sesame." Ideally situated at the foot of a sheltering mountain, the grounds boast a wealth of luxurious foliage. Not far from the entrance is a fine turreted mansion, at one time the residence of Lady Love Jones Parry, but now converted into an art gallery. There is a splen- did collection of pictures—many by eminent masters. Having inspected the hall, we ramble along shady paths arched with trees,until we see signposts with the direction" Path to the mountain." In a few minutes we are clear of the woods, and the ascent begins in earnest. A small stone rail is scaled, and then we trudge aloag over gorse and heather until the summit is gained: From such an elevation the visitor can see for many miles, and both landscape and marine views are glorious. Pwllheli, or as much as can be seen of it, looks almost like a small village in the distance. The breeze simply tears round one, and hats must be held down with both hands. The descent is easy enough, and we are soon seated in the cars again. To hear Lancashire spoken of at its true worth, one has to go to Wales: I got into conversation with a proud native of the Principality, and ventured to congratu- late him upon the beauties of Pvllhlj. "[hive seen many prettier places in England,' was his reply. I asked him to instance one, and was not a little sur- prised when he answered "Southport." Familiarity,

There is another happy gathering for tea, and of course the occasion cannot be allowed to pass without a little speech-making. Mr. Naylor is cheered to the echo, and in acknowledging the compliment cannot refrain from touching upon "foreign competition." The train is to leave at 7.4, so we have just time for a last look round the town. The day has been long, and many of the trippers, tired but happy, are seated in the train over half an hour before the homeward journey liegins. With the most pleasing of recollec- tions, we leave picturesque Pwllheli. The outing, we are all unanimous, has been a grand success. Soon rain sets in, but it only makes the trippers think how favoured they have been throughout the day. The run home is painfully slow. The poor engine really seems overburdened with its heavy freight, and there are several halts for "lubrication." The piers;are in darkness when we pass through Colwyn Bay and Rhyl—but most of the passengers are too sleepy to notice this. It is a delightful sensation to be patiently waiting on Walton Arches at 12-30 midnight for the mail to pass! One thinks "So near and yet so far." We steam into Bank Quay at 12.40, having been on our way fi.ye hours. This is, in fact the last excursion to returr.. Mr. Henshall smilingly watches the multitude descend to the subway. "They can all count the steps this morning," las remarks. They certainly could. E.I.

STEKTON HEATH V. WARRINGTON. Messrs. England and H. J. Westbrook

at Variance. ----- Stockton Hun an tha Warrington Light Railways.

[To THE EDITOR.] Sir,—The following two facts forma sufficient answer to Mr. England's letter and its enclosure, viz.: (1) It was expressly ruled by the Light Railway Com- missioners that the Stockton Heath Parish Council had a right to be heard; (2) the Parish Council were hoard and in subsequent negotiations with the Corporation they obtained terms which, so fares regards road-width, gave the Stockton Heath people a road 50 feet wide instead of one 45-feet wide, the width asked for by the County Council.—Yours, &c.

H. J. 'WESTBROOK. Stockton Heath, sth July, 1901.

[The above letter appeare'd. also in the "Guardian" on Wednesday and Mr. England has since forwarded the following reply.—En. "Ex."]

[To THE EDITOR.] The Widening of Old London Road.

Sir,—The kindness of Mr. H. J. Westbrook is simply crushing. Evidently not being satisfied with the answer that the Town Clerk of Warrington had beeft good enough to give me on the question of the widen- ing of Old London Road,he with more zeal than discre- tion, I suggest, rushes into the matter. I may say that personally I was quite satisfied with the answer that the Town Clerk gave me, as I was under the impression that in this matter he was the leading authority and that is why I referred to him. However it is apparent there is at least one person who is eagerly prepared to enter the lists and even to question the Town Clerk's opinion. Mr. Westbrook I hope will pardon me, when I say that I should not have thought of referring to his letter and not have thought of further pursuing the matter, if it had not been ope of public interest. In his letter he says the following two facts ' form a suffi- cient answer to Mr. England's letter and its enclosure. I must confess my surprise at Mr. West- brook's "two facts." We do not expect gentlemen of the learned profession—which I hope Mr. Westbrook some day will be a bright ornament—to fall into grievous errors of the nature he has done. I can only account for it on the ground of zeal and hurry. I may say at this juncture that I have courteously been allowed to see the documents and papers relating to this matter. _

Now what do I find with regard to Mr. Westbrook's "two facts " First of all with reference to No. 1. Although as the Town Clerk very cogently pointed out in his letter that the Light Railway Acts do not recognise a Parish Council. instead of it being as stated by Mr. Westbrook "that it was ruled they had a right" it was only by the courtesy of the Town Clerk of War- rington and the Light Railway Commissioners that the Parish Council were heard at all and it was simply granted as a" privilege." There is a great difference between " rights " and" privileges.' In fact parish councils have no locus staruli in matters of this kind and they are not even mentioned in the order granted at all, the term "local authority" referred to, meaning the Cheshire County Council and the Runcorn Rural District Authority. With reference Ito "Fact No. 2," on referring to the order I find that ! it is stated that the "metalled portion of the 'road shall not be less than 35 feet wide with a footpath not less than five feet on each side thereof i.e. road and footpaths inclusive not less than 45 feet wide." So much for Mr. Westbrook's " fact No. 2" Ein which Ile stated that "Oa* , Parish Council obtained terms which gave the Stockton I Heath people i road 50 feet wide instead of one 45 ' feet wide, the width asked for by the County Council." Nowhere in the order is 50 feet even mentioned and the road is not and will not be made 50 feet wide. I May I add that it is only fair and just that the credit of the widening of Old London-road should be given to , the Warrington Corp Oration and the Warrington and Northwich Light Railway whose enterprise it is to be hoped will eventually be crowned with success. May I also saw that it might have been vissible to have arranged for Old London-road to have been made GO feet wide, ' but when local bodies oppose schemes, then districts have to be satisfied with what is granted. It is sheer madness for the weaker body to adopt a dictatorial and opposition policy as has been demonstrated in Stockton Heath as well as elsewhere, but I am glad to say that symptoms have been observed since the last parish meeting of certain parish cour.cillors trimming their sails ready for a tack in another direction. In future a more ! reasonable policy will I think, be adopted.—Yours 1 faithfully,

E. ENGLAND.

13EFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

WA.RRINGTON MAN'S STRANGE CONDITCT.-CHARGING

INTO CROWDS ON HORSEBACK. When Daniel Taylor, greengrocer, 16, Paul-street, appeared in the dock at the Borough Police Court on Monday, the Chief Constable explained that there was a Tather formidable list of charges against the prisoner. Prom what Taylor's father had told him, he (Mr. Talbot) felt convinced that prisoner was, for the time being at all events, non compos mentis, and it was desirable that he should be placed under restraint. He would ask for the case to be adjourned for a fortnight, and in the meantime prisoner could be detained in a proper institution and have his mental state inquired into. Prisoner had been going about on horseback wildly charging into crowds, without any apparent reason.— Prisonm's father was called and) stated that he and his wife had been afraid of the prisoner. He was all right now.—On hearing this statement the Chief Constable proceeded with the case.—Prisoner was first charged with having been drunk in charge of a horse and trap in Arpley-road at 8-55 on Saturday night.—P.C. Clough proved the case and said that on being taken to the lock- up the prisoner acted like a lunatic.—Prisoner was then charged with having been drunk whilst in charge of a horse on June 26th, and with furiously riding the horse in Paul-street and Wilderspool-road on the same date. P.C. Corbett said he saw p!i.oner riding the horse very furiously near St. James's Church. He was waving his hat and shouting" I'm off to London, Hurrah !” Witness shouted to him to stop, but prisoner took no notice.— Chief Inspector Phillips stated that when prisoner was brought to the Police Station on Saturday night he was very noisy and acted almost like a lunatic.—P.C. Dyson spoke to seeing prisoner riding furiously in Paul-street. —Prisoner complained to the magistrates that the police would not allow him toleave the cell when he wanted.— From his manner, the Bench were satisfied that his state of mind ought to be inquired into, and sent him to the Workhouse for 14 days.

SECOND COURT.

Before Mr. H. Roberts (in the chair), Alderman Pier- point, Messrs. Hepherd, Pilling, Jolley,and Reynolds. "Six OF ONE AND HALF-A-DOZEN OF 'HE OTHER." —Maud Harris, Warrington View, Knutsford Road, Latchford, was summoned for assaulting Mary Noneley.— Mr. Steel represented the co mplai mint and Mr. Browne defended.—Mr. Steel said the parties were neighbours. On June 30th complainant remonstrated, with defendant's son who was throwing stones. Defendant thereupon rushed out, seized her by the hair, and dragged her to the ground.— Mr. Browne said it was a case of" six of one and half- a-dozen of the other."—After hearing evidence, the Bench dismissed the case.

A WALKING DAY SCENE.—John Ilennessey,3, Pocket- street, was summoned for assaulting Mary Ann Shaw, 6, Poqket-street, on July lst.—Mr. Steel appeared for the complainant and said that on the evening in question defendant came home under the influence of drink and used bad language about his wife. Mrs. Shaw asked him to desist, and without more ado he gave her a violent push and threw her on her back in the street. Later he made a rush at her, and sent her across the street with such force that her head came into contact with the setts, and a deep wound was inflicted.—Defendant said the complainant was drinking in his house when he came home, and he ordered her out. As she would not go, he ejected her, and she slipped and fell.—The case was dismissed.

A RAPID PERFORMANCE.—Henry Edward Riley, 27, Naylor-street, was charged with having deserted his wife, Margaret Riley, 29, Battersby-lane—Mr. Steel ap- peared for the complainant, and said the defendant had periodical spells of laziness or drunkenness, and would not attend to his work. He left his wife in May and had not been home or sent any money since. She had to go into the Workhouse, and the Guardians prosecuted the defendant who was sentenced to a month's imprison- ment. He came out of prison a fortnight last Satur- day, and had not provided a home or given complainant any monev.—The magistrates considered the defendant had not had sufficient time to provide a home, and ad- journed the case for a month. The Chairman said the case appeared to be a rather rapid performance. WENT TO AMERICA.—James Ross, Morris's-court, Church-street, was charged with deserting his wife, Myra Ross, 46, Sankey-street.--Mr. Browne appeared for the complainant, and said the parties had been married fifteen years. Three years last April defend- ant sold the seventh house of furniture they had dot together and went to America. He promised to send his wife El a week, but she had never received a penny from him. In her evidence, complainant said she worked in a tanyard for three months to maintain herself.—The magistrates granted a separation order with 10s. per week alimony, the wife to have the custody of the children. A guinea advocate's fee was allowed, the Chairman remarking "The man who runs away and leaves his wife and children for three years, deserves everything the law can give him." OnscENE LANGUAGE —Winifred Hennessey, Pocket- street, was summoned at the instance of Sarah Jane Shaw for making use of obscene language in Pocket- street on July 2nd.—Mr. Steel represented the corn- plainant.—A fine of ss. and costs was imposed.

GRAPPEN HALL.

HORSE KILLED BY LIGHTNING.—During the thunder storm last Saturday morning a horse, belonging to Mr. W. Bradford, was struck by lightning and killed. THE CRICKET CLUE.—LaSt week's match, for the Ist, was played at Lostock Gralam. Grappenhall were two men short so had to play substitutes. Winning the toss, the visitors decided to take first knock. The wicket was fiery but good, and runs came freely, the innings not closing until the score indicated 111. Towards this number, Burrows contributed a well-played 31. Warburton 26, Banks 14 and Ellis 10. The homesters responded with 89, time being up about a minute after the last wicket fell. Maudsley and Jakeman distin- guished themselves most for their side, the latter compiling 26, while the fornier was one less.l For the winners, Warburton again bowled very well, ihis analysis reading 7 wickets for 33 runs. Runcorn took 2 for 7. The 2nd at home, were visited by Altrin- cham St. Georges and once more drew. This time the draw was slightly in their favour. The score made by them totalled up to 70, while St. Georges bad put together 44 for 7 wickets. Thus 26 runs were wanted to make a tie, and only three batsmen to be disposed of Maile played a sound innings, for his useful 29 and England junior, in spite of absence from cricket managed to account for a dozen runs. The 2nd eleven have had quite a bad spell, but the last two matches give promise of a better turn. It is to be hoped they will emulate the senior eleven.

The Examiner, Saturday, July 9 1904

in common lodging-houses after the earlier cases of the year."

THE LESSONS OF THE BPIDE MIC.

The lessons to be drawn from this outbreak, Dr. Gornall says, are not unlike those of the epidemic of 1892-3 :—(1). In the present state of civilisation, or want of it, of the majority of the population nothing but the fact that they were mostly well vaccinated pre- vented an epidemic of wide-spread character, for cases were at large to our knowledge (and likely enough others without it) in the most infectious stage of the disease. It havbeen urged that the modifying effect of vaccination renders cases liable to be overlooked. This is more that compensated for by the check it puts on the reception of infection by others. (2) The re-vaccination of contacts in so thorough a fashion as we obtained prevented many more cases. Doubtless smallpox is most infectious in its later and scabbing stages, but it is not only then that it can be commum- eated.The early removal of most!of our smallpox patients tojhospital did no doubt helplaauch to prevent extension, but the value of this as contrasted with re-vaccination must be estimated in conjunction with a consideration of the Spoints II have ,mentioned under heading (3) Having regard to the generally vaccinated but only partially re-vaccinated condition of the population and to the difficulty of securing re-vaccination on a whole- sale scale during emergencies, without a smallpox hospital, Warrington would have been in as hopeless a condition in 1903 as it was during the epidemic of 1892. Only by means of satisfactory isolation of the cases that occurred were we able to complete the protection of the:main body of the populaticn, the rest of that pro- tection being made through the vaccination and disin- fection of contacts. It is clear, therefore, that (l) the provision of a smallpox hospital has been a great boon to the town (contrast the .2700 spent on this last out- break with the £lO,OOO that the 1892 epidemic cast). (2) If the general body of the population were by universal re-vaccination raised to the level of immunity obtained in this way in. case of those in contact with smallpox an epidemic would have scant chance of spreading. The waste of money (and I will not say energy) which these outbreaks of smallpox entail amply justify the demand for further developments in the way of compulsory re- vaccination. All vaccination arrangements ought as a matter of public health to be under the control and direction of tha Corporation, and nothing will be satis- factory that does not provide for a definite standard of successful vaccination. But the tramp question also demands attention, and not until it receives it will you be able definitely to determine upon putting to other uses the admirably situated hospital at Sankey, which seems so well adapted either as a convalescent home or as a place for treating cases of early phthisis. It has in some quarters (assuming tramps to be a peculiar strain in the population that propagates itself) been seriously proposed that they should be sterilized—which may be briefly described as Darwinism gone mad. Yet, had we (1) the power of compulsory re-vaccination of tramps at the discretion of the Medical Officer of Health ;(2) the power to secure that areas where smallpox exists should be declared infected and no tramps allowed to leave them except under proper guarantees, the diffusion of smallpox would soon come to an end.

ME &SLES. THE CLOSING OF SCHOOLS.

Eighty-three notifications of this disease were re- ceived during the first quarter of the year; 1,543 during the remainder; or 1,626 in all ; besides this the Medical Officer also received information of many cases from the Education Office. Each of these entailed at least one visit, many more than one, by officers of the Health Department, the vast majority being seen to by the 1 idy inspector. Necessarily, in combination with her numerous other duties, says the Medical Officer, it was impossible that she could give more than cursory attention to each case, though, as far as it went, her work was undoubtedly of some use, if only as tending to educate the mothers of the town into taking a more serious view of measles than before. Reviewing the precautions taken at the schools, Dr. Gornall says what was attempted was: (1) to exclude all children infected vfith measles for at least a month; (2) to exclude all brothers and sisters for three weeks; (3) to extend the periods of exclusion if further cases occur • (4) in case of a considerable prevalence of measles in a particular school, to disinfect the premises, including desks, slates, and drinking vessels But other desirable measures were not carried out, con- tinues Dr. Gornall, because of their impossibility, (1) to afford hospital isolation in necessary instances; (2) to disinfect the houses, clothing, and persons of children who had had or been in contact with measles. This doubtless detracted from, if it did not altogether neutralize, the value of the action actuilly taen. Had there been room in the hospital I should cert,anly have. urged upon the committee the desirability of taking into hospital the cases most in need of it, but even then we could only have dealt with a comparatively small pro- portion of the thousands of children who had measles. Still less was it within my power to provide for the requisite measures of disinfection. Yet without these latter such action as was taken can be of little Measles raged with increating virulence throughout the year. and the somewhat hesitating measures I took to close the schools were a rather confession of failure to deal with the epidemic than any part of a serious attempt to copa w;th it. I do not complain of the attitude taken up by certain members of the Education Commitpe, who had pro- bably little practical experience of dealing with measles epidemics, but Ido claim that the Medical Officer of Health should have a freehand to try experiments : such powers given to him in Case of sun] !pox have not been abused. Moreover, the question of measles has under the ne v Education Act acquired such filaacial impor- tance that the town must in the end feel the benefit of any success in tackling it, quite apart from the saving of life and the prevention of the many ill consequences of the disease, which not only results in eye and ear affections, but very strongly predisposes some children to tuberculous affections. I am prepared without hesitation to admit that we as yet know very little of the real nature of this disease, and shall be the last to dogmatiz on a matter, on which some with little or no knowledge of the subject seam to have no difficulty in forming an opinion.__

SCARLET FEVER. 110 W IT IS SPREAD.

In 1903 the scarlet fever cases, 289 in number, were fairly evenly distributed throughout the town, and it W&3 not possible, as in the previous year, to point out any marked foci of infection. Of course in individual cases some history of contact with a person with scarlet fever was often obtained, but, at any rate in the last quarter of the year, the poison of the disease was fairly well distributed throughout the town. One consider- able outbreak of 16 cases among the 83 papas in the Clergy Daughters' school, which was dealt with by sending the healthy girls home and turning the place into a hospital for the time being' could not in any way be accounced for. Sundry small defects in the drains ' were discovered, but were not, I think, responsible for what occurred; the origin of the milk supply was carefully investigated (I took a ;journey out to a farm in Appleton on purpose), but with no result, and in the end ' the only origin that could, be suggested was that one of the girls had acted as a " carrier ' case, bringing the infection from home, or some source of infection out- side the town, with her, and so started the epidemic, ' which began some weeks after the return from the summer holidays. In a disquisition on the causes of scarlet fever, Dr. Gornall discusses the question of how the poison spreads from person to person. It undoubtedly does so, he says, and probably in this way the organisms swarm on the surface of the throat, and in the many recesses of the lung membrane of the throat and nose where they originally got in, and every time a person in such a state coughs or sneezes he distributes about the room minute drops of sputum in which is the cause of scarlet fever ; it is contained too in the nasal discharges, which is fff) conunla in this disease, appearing in severe cases at the beginning, though often manifesting itself later. Hence, the danger of close. contact, as occurs when children kiss one another, sleep in the same bed, or (indirectly) drink out of the same cup. In this conndction an observation by our removal officer is of interest. He has noticed that the houses from which it is commonest to have second and third cases are those in which the first Case is found (most likely on a couch) in the kitchen, with the other chil- dren in the roofn. His experience leads him to look upon secondary cases as less probable when the child is found in a bedroom, and separated from the rest of the family. Forty per cent of the houses in Warrington contain four rooms or less. The people living in these houses are poor, and not generally well informed on the necessity o: Isolation. Moreover, the greater num- ber of cases of scarlet fever is among this class of the population. The danger of leaving such patients at home is manifest.

THE HEALTH OF NE BOROUGH.

REPORT OF THE MEDICAL OFFICER.

Dr. J. G. Gornall, M.A., M. 8., medical officer of heaith for the Borough, has just issued his annual report for 1903.

A TRIBUTE TO THE STAFF,

"I have always attempted in writing reports to do so," says the Medical Officer, "in a truly scientific spirit, and to avoid all those methods of advertisement, which are so common in the commercial community around us,but are quite out of .place in the report of a Medical Officer of Health. But on this occasion I cannot refrain from expressing some satisfaction at the results of our efforts, I of which an account is given in dealing with small-pox. It is no doubt: true that we might have done even better had we carried out the same plan in its entirety from the beginning of the outbreak, but some limit to the working capacity of the sanitarystaff will no doubt be admitted even by those who are most critical of public officials. What, however, I want people to realze is—that the really hard work of the smallpox outbreak fell on the staff, of the Public Health department and of the hospitals, and that by their efforts in detecting cases. and so preventing the spread of the disease, and their energy in carrying out measures of disinfection and in removing patients, working often into the small hours of the morning, great expense was saved to the town. It is no economy in the long run to have a public health department either understaffed or underpaid as, I fear, some people would like to see it. I have indeed not only to call your attention to the very satisfactory way in which the members of the staff have worked, but to express my own indebtedness to them. My thanks are also due to the medical profes- sion of the town for their ready co-operation with the Health Department, and especially am I grateful for the untiring energy of the public vaccinators during our recent troubles. But not only of their services is some recognition owing. The Education Committee enabled us to deal very effectively with small-pox cases in the schools—and the employers of labour in War- rington stood by us well—while to others I am thankful for professional assistance during a time of special stress. To you, sirs (the members of the Health Com- mittee) for your continued kindness to nie, and especi- ally because you have, by appointing a public analyst, helped towards the grealization of part of the scheme which I recently ut before you, I desire to express my sincere gratitude."

A FEW COMMENTS.

In discussing the birth rate of the Borough, Dr. Gornall says:"Warrington remains with one of the highest birth rates among the urban districts; a fact which. taken with the generally healthy and well-nourished appearance of ‘Varrington children, would seem to show that the much talked of physical degeneration has not yet made much inroad here. Still it remains true that the birth-rate declines from year to year, as in the rest of the country; pro- bably here as elsewhere, the diminution of families is most marked among the more provident and better- instructed classes of the community, and in so far as the low birth-rate among these people is a matter of calculation, it is an indication either of an entirely wholesome and unselfish line of conduct in marital re- lations, or of the existence of a condition of things which is from every aspect deplorable." Referring to the death-rate, Dr. Gornall says it compares very un- favourably with that of 1902—but then that was the lowest on record (16.6) and belonged to a year whose climatic conditions were specially favourable. The diseases which mainly contributed to the increase were as follows :—Nfeasles 97 deaths as compared with 1 deaths in 1902, whooping cough 30 deaths as compared with 15 and diarrhcea 85 deaths as compared with 51.

TIIE SM ILLPDX EPIDEMIC,

Dr. Gornall devotes 25 pages to the smallpox epidemic Warrington is nlw, he says, in a peculiarly favourable position with ruard to smallpox; having on the one hand a hospit*two miles outside the town in a sparsely populated district and on the other a well-vaccinated population. Moreover p Mlle opinion is well informed of the danger of letting smallpox get a hold, and quite prepared to back up the Health Department in any reasonable measures for its prevention. In dis- cussing the vaccination returns, Dr. Gornall has a sarcastic word for the anti-vaccinator. It will be seen from ths returns,he says, that a eery high percentage of the children who survive to four months of age are vaccinated, and that the species "conscientious objector" is not on the increase. Thus, in 1902, 97 per cent of the possible infants were vaccinated, and only eight of them were possessed of parents with a "con- science." AS TO THE PROGRESS OF THE EPIDEMIC,

Dr. Gornall says that it was mainly confined to one district, partly in St. John's, though mostly in the Orford Ward, and though a few instances occurred out- side these limits, the outbreak was from the beginning lc well under control, and the people generally so alive to the danger of concealment, that no harm came from these. 119 persons were removed during the period dealt with-78 during 1903 and 31 during the first two months of 1904. Of these, 107 had smallpox ; 9 were cases at first doubtful, but subsequently shewn not to be smallpox: 5 were infants for whom we could find no place elsewhere. None of the infants removed, as above mentioned, took the disease ; but one, already much emaciated from a wasting illness,died of marasmus. After the visit of the Local Government Inspector, and his advise of the provision of a shelter for the housing and disinfection of the persons of the contacts, it was decided to utilize the small pavilion at Aikin-street for the purpose the only place available at once. These measures entailed a very considerable labour, indeed to everybody concerned, for between December 19th and the cessation of the outbreak, at least 200 persons were bathed and had their clothes disinfected: food was pro- vided for these people when they had to wait long. Dr. Gornall describes the plan adopted to deal with the epidemic, which is onthe following lines :—(1) Immedi- ately a case is reported by a medical practitioner, or discovered by one of the Sanitary Inspectors, who are constantly on the look out, it is removed to the Sanatorium at Sankey. (2) Thereupon an inspector visits the hou.se and obtains a complete list of the residents, and any other persons who have been in con- tact, with their addresses, and their places of employ- ment or schools. (3) The same inspector takes this list Ito the public vaccinator, who invariably endeavours to vaccinate or re-vaccinate them all the same day. (4) The same day, also, notices are sent to the various places of employment of the persons discovered in con- tact, requesting that they be not allowed to return until a certificate of freedom from infection is received from the Medical Officer of Health. "This plan," continues Dr. Gornall," though following in the main the lines on which we had worked from the beginning, grew in exactitude as we went. In my belief it affords the best means of dealing with a threatened epidemic in such a place as Warrington when the disease has invaded private houses. I shall certainly carry it out in any future emergency of the kind. It would be far different if we had simply to deal with cases in common lodging-houses among tramps and persons of no definite employment, but not a single case occurred ,

===== 0002601_19040709_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
an exaggerated impression of the foulness of the air in some instances, but it is, I think, desirable that means for testing the chemical composition of the air in the schools, such as are now beino, used in connection with factories and workshops, should be brought into use before Anything final is settled. . _

Dr. Got:nall Makes the following recommendations in regard to schools: (I.) The maintenance of the exist- ing means of ventilation; (2) The flushing out of all rooms in the intervals between hours of occupation ; (3) Further inspections of the schools, and examination of air, to ascertain the degree of fouling of thP air and the need of improvement in the ventilating arrange- ments; (4) The reduction of overcrowding; (5) The abolition of pail closets at schools; (6) The introduction in all schools of a more satisfactory type of urinal; (7) The reform of the existing lavatories. THE WORK OF THE FEMALE INSPECTOR.

The work of Miss Hoyle, appointed an inspector in the Public Health Department, in October, 1902 has, observes Dr. Gornall, considerably developed. Unfor- tunately there is too much for one woman to do, if it is to be done with any thoroughness, but as far as she _ _

has been able, Miss 1141 e has 'carrie'd out with great energy and a zeal tempered by discretion the duties for which she was appointed. There is evidence from sources outside this department that very considerable benefit has accrued to the town from the wise step which the committee took in appointing her. I refer more particularly to the report of the local branch of the Society for Preventing Cruelty to Children. lam also told that there has of late been an improvement in the cleanliness of children attending public schools—for which I do not claim all the credit for this department —but it is not unlikely that the work that has been done by it has been auxiliary to other influences leading to the same desirable end. UNPAVED STREETS AND PAsSAGES.

This is a subject, the Medical Officer remarks, which has been repeatedly brought to the notice of the Health Oom•nittee. The dangers to health connected with the want of a proper surface to the ground in the neigh- bourhood of dwellings are manifold. To children especially must it be inimical; the daily trudging to school through slush, and pools of water, and the fact that in rainy seasons they have only the streets in such a state of dampness to play in, if not the actual cause of disease, most certainly conduce to it. Another drawback is the absolute impossibility of sweeping and cleansing unpaved streets and passages, and a perhaps still more serious one is the slopping of the contents of pails, which is bound to occur occasionally, and then fouls the surface and upper layers of the soil with matter possibly of an infeatious nature. Little or no improvement can be observe I in the rate of securing the pavements of streets and still less of passages •, the latter are possibly the more dangerous of the two, The list given of those that require attention contains many names which appeared in a similar list published in 1898, and some even were mentioned by the Medical Officer of Health twenty years ago and remain unpaved to this day. It is no part of my business to indicate the causes of this unsatisfactory state of affairs, or to apportion the blame for it to anyone, but I desire to draw the attention of the Health Committee to this want of paving as one of the most serious insanitary conditions at present existing the town. . .

The list referred to is a tabulation of all the unpaved -streets and passages in the Borough, and covers eight printed pages of the report.

VEGETABLES UP-TO-DATE.

The strict vegatarian and all others who appreciate the very useful part which vegetables play in our daily dietary, must often wish for a means of making the homelier vegetables more appetising. A simple and most successful way to accomplish this is to serve the vegetables with an appstising sauce. Such sauces are e isily made with the aid of Brown and Poison's "Patent" Corn Flour. I Brown Sr. Poison's publish a special recipe book entitled "Fish and Vegetable Sauces," a copy of Nvhicti will be forwarded to every lady sending a Id stamp to B. & P., Paisley. The recipes include sauces for even the most common vegetables in daily use, such as carrots. turnips,cabbage, ctc., and to serve such vegetables with a good sauce gives them an added relish.

TRIP DAY.

WORKS EXCURSIONS FROM WARRINGTON.

Saturday was what is now familiarly known as "trip day" in Warrington, when the usual excursions are run to pleasant seaside and holiday resorts. Below will be found particulars of how the day was spent.

===== 0002601_19040709_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. R. A. NAYLOR'S EMPLOYEES AT PWLLHELI. Mr. Naylor and Foreign Competition.

The seventeenth annual excursion of the employes at Howley Timber Mills will rank as one of the most suc- cessful ever held. Pwllheli—one of the celebrated Carnarvon boroughs—was visited, and the weather being glorious the outing was thoroughly enjoyed. On arriving at 9.30 an excellent breakfast was served, and the par.ty, numbering nearly 500, dispersed to onjoy themselves in a variety of ways. Most of the trippers made the journey by car to Llanbedrog and rambled through the picturesque Glen- y-Weddw grounds and inspected the fine art gallery. At five o'clock tea was partaken of. Mr. Thomas Egerton proposed "Success to Howley Mills," coupled with the name of Mr. R. A. Naylor, to whom he proposed a hearty vote of thanks for his hospitality. He referred to the business abilities of the employers, the energy of the managers and the active co-operation of the workmen which had made the firm one of the most successful in the kingdom. Mr. Louis Cross seconded, as one of the oldest "hands." He pointed out that that was the 17th or 18th annual excursion.

The motion was heartily agreed to, cheers being enthusiastically given for Mr. R. A. and Miss Naylor and Mr. John Naylor, who was unable to be present. Mr. Naylor, in reply, said one of their friends had intimated that that was the 17th excursion ; and he (Mr. Naylor) hoped they would live to see 17 more. (Cheers.) Certainly the gathering that day was one of the best, and he was sure he was reiterating their sentiments when he made the observation that they had never enjoyed an excursion better. (Hear, hear.) They were visiting one of the celebrated Carnarvon boroughs— Pwllheli. There were five more of them—Conway, Bangor, Carnarvon. Criccietb, and Nevin, and, possibly, they might take these places for their excursion in the years to come. They had always made a point at Howley of never making any difference between the working men and those called the "bosses of the establishment " —(laughter)—and if a concern was to prosper in these days of keen competition there must be no friction, but Iperfect unanimity of feeling and of action. (Hea,r,hear.) He would not like to be connected with any concern if that could not be achieved, and he trusted that the very happy relations which had hitherto existed between them would always continue. (Hear, hear.) Some- one had remarked in proposing that vote of thanks that the success of Howley Mills and the trade connected with it was owing to the fact that they had worked well together, all pulling in the same direction. And that was what they ought to do. If it had been otherwise they could not have stood against the unfair foreign competition to which they were subjected, and ihe might tell them that for every pound they paid in 'wages they could get the same work done in the same manner for 12s. 6d. by foreign workmen. (A Voice: " Not so good, sir," and laughter.) A gentleman had said "Not so good." That might be so,but he could tell them distinctly that 20 years ago he had a business offered to him across the North Sea.a business hecould have made thousands of pounds out of, but he had con- tinued to employ British labour and to invest the capital he had in the United Kingdom. (Applause.) Now the time had arrived when the workingmen of the country would have to look after their own interests. Why should a man be penalized because he was a patriotic British subject? Why should a man be permitted to make more money because he went across the water, invested his capita there, and employed cheap labour? Many firms in this country had sold up their establishments and had gone abroad in order that they might supply the requirements of those countries and be in a position also to retain the trade they had been accustomed to do within the United Kingdom. That was not Lir, and sooner or later they would have to do something to remedy the mischief that was being done. But that was not the occasion to talk about a subject such as this. He wished to say that his sister and himself were very glad to be present, and to see the employees enjoy themselves. They had passed through the worst winter for trade that had ever been known within living memory. Fortunately they had done better than other people, and had had rather more than their fair share of work, and they would try to do their utmost to keep the men and boys well and honestly employed. (Hear, hear.) The development of the business had been gradual, but they hoped to be able before long to make it a still greater success. If they only knew how to accomplish that one end they would ask for the co-operation of all and say, " Let every boy, every man, every manager and everyone financially interested in the business have a fair and honest share of the plunder." (Applause.) He hoped before long to increase the area of their business trans- actions,and he trusted that that would be to the interests of all of them. (Applause.) Miss Naylor said she also thanked all present for their hearty welcome. She wished to inform them that before very long she hoped to see everyone present that day at a very good tea at HowleyeMills. (Cheers.) Leavinc, Pwllheli at 7.45, Warrington was reached, after a teliousjourney, at 12.40. A special article descriptive of this excursion appears in another column.

===== 0002601_19040709_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
WILLIAM ROSERTSON LIMITED, LATCHFORD.

The second annual pic-nic from the above Company's Works took place on Saturday, the chosen place this time being Rhyl. The party of nearly 100 left Bank Quay Station at 6.30 a.m., and after a good run, arrived at Rhyl about 8 o'clock. The weather though not very bright, was warm, and showed 'signs of a fine day. During the morning the party divided to visit different places of interest, such as the Marble Church at Bodelwyddan, and the Cathedral at St. Asaph, passing on the way the house where Edward I. held his parlia- ment in the year 1283, whilst others enjoyed a walk along the magnificent promenade, or the boating on the Marine Lake. Dinner was served at 11,in the sumptuous restaurant of the Queen's Hotel, and great praise is due to Mr. and Mrs. Bartlett, the manager and manageress respectively, for the splendid arrangements they had made, and for their most creditable arraniTement of the function, which gave the greatest satisfaction to all The party were highly pleased to have with them Mr. Robertson, the Chairman and managing director of the Company, and Mrs. Robertson M. Wareing, the manager, and Mrs. Wareing • Mr. Collins, the secretary and Mrs. Collins; and dr. Sherratt, the assistant manager, and Mrs. Sherratt. Dinner being over, Mr. , Robertson addressed a few well-chosen words to the ' company, wishing them a very enjoyable day, and they then dispersed to visit other places of interest, such as the Waterfalls at Dyserth, Ste. The bulk of the party assembled at 2.30 in the afternoon at the Queen's Palace Ball-room, to witness the special performance which had been provided for them, or to indulge in dancing on the famous parquet floor (which, being laid on no less Ithan 2,500 springs, was recently described in an article in one of • the popular periodicals as one of the best floors in the world) and to the accompaniment of the ' renowned orchestra, under the direction of the accom- plished director and leader, Mons. Bosanquet. The entertainment which was put on the boards was of the usual high character provided at the Rhyl Palace, and was well appreciated, as also were the other attractions of the Palace,— Venice, the Tower, the Palm Gardens, drz., &c. The return journey was commenced at about nine o'clock and Warrington was reached by ten minutes to eleven, all the party arriving home safely, with the memories of a happy day. In connection with this picnic may be mentioned that on Thursday after the close of the works, Mr. Wareing, on behalf of the employees, presented Mr. Collins with a beautiful gold mounted umbrella, and Mr. Humphries with a choice silver mounted meerschaum pipe, and an elegant tobacco pouch, each bearing the initials of the recipients, as a small token of appreciation of their services as hon. treasurer and hon. secretary respective- ly for the sick club, savings bank and picnics. CORPORATION EMPLOYES AT SOUTHPORT.

Many of the employees of the Corporation and friends spent the day at Southport. The train left the Central Station at 6.45 and returned from Southport at 8,30, Warrington being reached at 9.30. i The day was agree- ably spent in a variety of ways, including boating on the marine lake,visits to the Hesketh Park and the Winter Gardens, and driving to Birkdale, while some took ad- vantage of the four hours' sail to Blackpool and back. The weather was favourable, there being only one shower throughout the day.

SANKEY BROOK AGAIN.

Mr. THOMPSON said the stench from Sankey Brook was very bad Weed.

Mr. ItNOWLES : The brook wants cleaning out. Mr. ILERFOOT : That matter has been discugsed many times, but an enormous expenditure would be involved.

- The Surveyor read a letter from Messrs. Mortimer and Company stating that they had let the contract for the cleaning out of a ditch at Orford which empties into Sankey Brook. The Surveyor said the work would be no good, as the firm was still turning refuse into the ditch.

Mr. Kprfoot said the grievance was a very old one. On one occasion he and Dr. Sephton interviewed the late Mr. Mortimer who said be would abate the nuisance, at whatever cost, if the Council would only show him how that was to be done.

On the motion of Mr. Knowles, it was decided to serve a notice upon the firm to abate the nuisance.

NOTICE ON A COUNCILLOR.

Several other notices were Ordered to be served, including one upon Mr. R. Fotshaw, the representative of Burtonwood in respect of the pollution of the brook at Burtonwood: BUTTERMILK BRIDGE IN A DANGEROUS CONDITION.

Mr. THOMPSON said the repairing of this bridge had not been done to his satisfaction or to the satisfaction of the people of the neighbonrhood. The SURVEYOR said the work was not finished. The bridge had been made safe temporarily. Mr. THOMPSON: I was there on Sunday night, and I was rather afraid that I might go through. The CHAIRMAN said he thought that when the Roads Committee went round the district they ought to make a special visit to the bridge. For the safety of the public, a new iron bridge should be provided. The SURVEYOR said the BorOugh Surveyor was re- cently approached, but the Corporation declined to join with the District Council in erecting a new bridge. The CHAIRMAN: I th ink the Corporation would comply with our wishes if the matter were brought before the Town Council.

FEARNHEAD SEWAGE.

A report on the Fearnhead Sewerage Scheme was sub- mitted by the Surveyor. The Council decided that copies should be printed and forwarded to each member of the authority and to the Parish Council.

Central Station Clerical Staff Picnic. On Walking Day the clerical staff at the Central. Station had a picnic to Llandudno. The party, num- bering 20, proceeded in a saloon provided by the London and North-Western Railway Company. On arrival they enjoyed themselves as inclination dictated until lunch, which was partaken of at Mr. Sumner's Grosvenor Restaurant,Mostyn-street. Then all joined the North Wales Steamship Co.'s boat, St. Elvies and sailed to Menai Bridge. A short ramble was enjoyed, and on returning to Llandudnq, tea was served at the same cafe. Warrington was reached at 9.30 p.m. after a successful outing.

BISHOP OF MANCHESTER AT WARRINGTON.

Prize-Giving at the Training College.

I The Bishop of Manchester (Dr. Knox) on Saturday distributed prizes to the students of the Warrington Training College. The students were accommodated on a platform, and there was a large and distinguished 1 company in the body of the hall. The chair was occu- pied by the Bishop of Manchester, and he was accom- panied on the platform by the Dean of Manchester (Dr. Maclure), the \Ten. Archdeacon Barber, Canon Willis, Penrhyn, Kelly and Gore, Mr. R. H. Joynson aid the Principal (Rev. Morley Stevenson.)
