Williams, Mr. and Mrs. Winstanley, Miss Vvrenn, Mr. and Mrs. Yates and all the members of the Resident Staff. ADDRESS BY THE PRINCIPAL. THE DEMAND FOR TEACHERS.

The Rev. MORLEY STEVENSON, in opening the pro- ceedings, said he did not think anyone could fail to notice that the Education Act of 1902, whatever might be its merits or demerits, had awakened throughout the country a very great wave of interest in popular educa- tion. He thought that those of them who had been interested in education, perhaps for many years, could not recall any period in which the interests of the country at large had been so keenly awakened, and so largely aroused in the question as it had been during the I past year, and amongst other things that interest had led to the great question of the supply of properly trained teachers. On all sides they heard that there was a scarcity of well-trained teachers, and on all sides also they saw that people were now at last beginning to awaken to the fact that the supply and training of teachers was really the key to the whole educational problem. That was very gratifying to them in training colleges. They had for many years been trying to awaken an interest in the work of training collegesand although they had succeeded, perhaps, to a certain extent—with such audiences as he was then addressing and with those who were really interested in the question of educa- tion—be was sure they would all be astonished if they realized at all the depth of the ignorance and the slight- ness of tke interest which the public of this great country at large had felt in the question of the supply and training of teachers. Whatever faults might be attributed to that college he did not think they could be accused of having been blind to the fact that the supply of teachers was not what it ought to have been, or that they had not tried their very best to increase that supply. Last year he had the pleasure of telling them that they were opening—as they had now opened—a large hostel at Orford lianas an addition to the college, in which accommodation for thirty students had been found. (Hear, hear.) He had now the pleasure of adding to that, that owing largely to the generosity of one who had always taken a great in- terest in the college they would be enabled, they hoped, in September, to open another hostel in Manchester. (Applause.) In that hostel they hoped to place those students—they would not be a large number—who would be able to devote three years to their training instead of two, and who were capable of profiting by a University course and working for a University degree. Therefore they would see that the Church of England, as represented In this part by that college,had done its best to provide for the Increase in the number of teachers, and to offer to those who were intellectually Most gifted and capable of profiting by it, the opportunity of having the highest educational advantages which could be put within their reach. (Applause.) He thought he must say a word of warning, and as he had his back to the students he could say it all the more comfortably. (Laughter.) It was with reference to the effect that this great and sudden demand for trained teachers was likely to have upon the teachers themselves, and he wished to point out that there was a very distinct danger to the teachers. There was a great demand for them and a correspond- ing increase in salaries, and they were able to pick and choose. But the great demand for them and their being so much sought after made them run the risk of getting their heads turned. He hoped the teachers—and he was not referring merely to his own young teachers, but to the teachers all over the country—would rise to the seriousness and responsibility of their office and not be flitting about from one school to another, as a butterfly went from one flower to another. because there was a little more honey to be gained in the shape of a few pounds extra here or there. (Hear, hear.) The work of the teacher was so important, and her influence so great,that it should not be jeopardized by constant changes for the sake of some small good of this world, but they should feel that by earnest, steadfast, persevering work in one sphere they would best realize the greatest and brightest hopee they had for them and most successfully carry out that important work which the country looked to them to do. (Applause.) it was to the training colleges that the country looked chiefly and mainly for the supply of the best adapted and best trained teachers The colleges, as they were doubtless aware, were of two kinds—residential and day. He referred to this matter 'because lately there seemed to have been a sort of current of feeling about it, which had no doubt been set in motion in certain quarters, that the residential college was to be looked upon as an old, deficient, and rather effete institution, and that the day college was to be the college of the future. He did not wish for a moment to enter into a comparison of the two, and he did not think it would be becoming of him to do so. Also he had the greatest respect for the work of the day colleges. But certain ' charges had been brought against residential colleges and those charges had been made by those who probably, he should think,' had never been inside a residential college in their lives. They were told that residential colleges were very narrow; that the students, which was obvious, were all for the same profession; and that they were shut up and located in a sort of prison house—(laughter)—and had no opportunity of mixing with other people, and consequently were turned out after some mechanical pattern in a particular groove to run their particular course of life. He wished to say, as emphatically as possible.that after more than 20 yeers' experience in the college he did not think there was an atom of truth in such charges. (Applause.) The whole course of study and social life of the college, the frequency with which students wore brought into contact with great men—great lecturers, great teachers, great preachers— all tended to vary and widen the life, and he could appeal with confidence to any of the students who had passed through the course of their college life there and be sure that the 3 would maintain what he said and tell him that the effect of the college career had been immensely to widen intellectually, and refine and culture the life with which they went out to face the world. (Applause).

===== 0002601_19040709_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
'THE BISHOP AND RELIGIOUS TRAINING

The LORD BISHOP, who was cordially received, said it behoved a Bishop of Manchester to be somewhat interested in education, for one of his first experiences when he come into his diocese was that he had to preach school sermons every Sunday morning and evening for the first half-year at all events of his existence. (Laughter.) And when he had done that for half a year he must indeed know a great deal about education if he had anything new to say to an audience like the present. He did not flatter himself that he had much that was new to say. He had had something to say, but he was very nnwi e to let the Principal speak before him— (laughter)—as he had said most of the things it was his intention to say, and said them with his experience behind him and with his wonderful exactness and taste- ' fulness of language. Addressing himself more particu- larly to tbe students, his lordship said they who were being trained there were looking forward to going out. very shortly, and the goal they had before them was some day of having a school of their own, which they could really call their own, and in which they could carry out those magnificent ideas which, he had no doubt, they had been thinking over and dreaming about in the hope one day of realizing. Ho trusted they would all have schools of their own—except those whom the malignity of the other sex carried off— (laughter)—and thus robbed them of a great portion of the fruit of their labours,but they no doubt contributed thereby to the happiness of a great many homes and provided the schools by and by with some excellent scholars. (Loud laughter.) But meanwhile they must

===== 0002601_19040709_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Saturday, July 9, 1904.

confess the fact that there was a leakage, and allow for the leakage, but what most of them hoped for was cer- tainly to haven school. When they had got that school. he thought one of the first things that would dawn upon them was that some of the regulations and requirements of the college, which had, perhaps, appeared to have been a little pedantic and old-fashioned would be found to have some meaning after all, and they would then thank those whom they had perhaps dubbed old-fogies— ,(laughter)—for having insisted nponthem. After refer- ring to the importance of the students acquiring the power to rule themselves and the power to rule other people, the Bishop, speaking on day and residential training'colleges, said that whatever opinions might be held upon the subject, the residential training college was bound to stay, and was hound to become one of their great strongholds for national education. The value of residential colleges lay in the intercourse which it afforded the students with one another—the common life, the common home and, for those who valued it, that religious instruction which helped to build up character hi a way that it could be built up by no other educational system. It gave them powers of self-government, of dealing with other people and of ruling which, he was confident, no day college had ever yet imparted or ever would impart. Another reason why they greatly valued their residential colleges was that they gave definite religious instruction according to the principles of the Church of England. "And about this definite instruction in the principles of the Church of England," the Bishop said, "let me say we are sometimes mis- understood. I have heard even members of my own Church and clergy speaking in a way which seems to me entirely to misunderstand what we are trying to do. For instance, I have heard it said, 'How ungrateful it is of those who have received a college training in our residential colleges that when they grow up and go out into the world, in a few years some of them cease to be.• members of the Church of England. and others are very loosely attached,' and so on. 'Surely,' they say, 'grati- tude should bind them to be strong, ardent Churchmen and Churchwomen all the rest of their lives.' That seems simply and entirely a mistaken view of what a residential col4ege is aiming at. We do not ask you to come here and, before you can understand all the diffi- culties of. the questions which will be propounded to you, to be bound down to a system, with all that it entails, and say you are not to go beyond the limits of this system all the rest of your days.. We do not want to prejudice or to hamper you, to fetter you with shackles that are to be lifelong. That is wholly absent from, at all events, mv cemception of a residential training college, and if I thought that was what we meant by definite religious instruction, I, for one, would have nothing to do with a residential training college, for I hold it to be of the utmost importance that all young people should be learners and seekers after truth. It seems to me that 1 what we do here is to put before you that which we hold to be true and which we are convinced to be true, and we say, as we teach it, 'Work out this thing for yourselves, think out and live out what we teach you, and do it with the single desire of arriving at the truth in the sight of God. Have no fear of the consequences if you are earnest seekers after truth.' We do not give definite religious instruction inour residential training colleges with the idea of fettering the free minds of young people at an early stage of their existence, but in the hope of helping them to arrive at the truth."— (Hear, hear). He was glad (the Bishop went on to say) to have an opportunity of saying publicly that it had come to his knowledge that it was supposed they gave up an undue proportion of time in Church dayand resi- dential training colleges to religious instruction. That was not the case. They did not give more time to religious instruction than was given in undenominational training colleges. •

WARRINGTON RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL.

The Longford Depot Nuisance.— Notice

to Abate.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Rural Dis- trict Council was held at Bewsey Chambers on Wed- nesday afternoon Mr. T. Halton presiding.—There were also present Murray, Messrs. Brookes, Mather, Backhous' e Knowles, Houghton, Ball, Thompson, Long- ton, with Dr. Sephton (medical officer of health), S. Sutton (assistant clerk), F. W. Kerfoot (inspector), B. S. Newcombe (surveyor), and H. H. Burnage (assistant surveyor).

===== 0002601_19040709_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
RE-APPOINTMENTS,

The Local Government Board wrote sanctioning the re-appointments of Dr. Sephton and Mr. Kerfoot for the ensuing year at s 'Juries of £l5O each.

WATER SUPPLY QUESTIONS.

The Council sealed an agreem-ent with the Warring- ton Corporation for the supply of water to Brow Farm, Great Sankey.

The Warrington Water Engineer (Mr. Deas) wrote respecting the proposed extension of the water main to several farms at Burtonwood. He stated that in future all expenses in the township must be guaranteed at the same percentage as was charged on all the out- lying townships. This percentage at the present time was 8 per cent on the cost of the work for a term of seven years. The ASSISTANT CLERK said that as far as Burtonwood was concerned there was an advance of from 5 to 8 per cent on the cost. The original charge was 4 per cent. but the Corporation afterwards said they had made a mis- take and raised it to 5 per cent. The point to be settled was whether the Council was willing to pay the increased charge of 8 per cent. It was decided that arrangements be made for the supply of the water as requested.

===== 0002601_19040709_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
PENKETH DUST CONTRACT.

MT. THOMAS WHITFIELD, the dust contractor at Penketh, wrote asking that his remuneration should be increased by £l5 which would make it .£loo—the amount received by his predecessor. The application. was referred to the Penketh Parish Council.

A RESIGNATION

At the last meeting of the special committee on bye- laws a letter was read from Mr. Frank R. Bickford, tendering his resignation of the office of junior assist- ant surveyor, as he had been offered another position. He asked to be allowed to leave the Council's service at once and expressed his willingness, if necessary, to forfeit the month's salary. - _

The committee recommended that Mr. Bickford, be allowed to leave without the forfeiture of the salary, and the Council agreed to this course.

REVISION OF BYELA.WS.

At a meeting of the Byelaws Revision Committee, it was decided that the byelaws now in force be continued without operation, and several of the model byelaws drawn up by the Local Government Board were adopted. The committee's decision was approved. HEALTH OF THE DISTRICT.

Dr. SEPTITON reported that during the month there had been 28 births and 52 deaths. The death rate from all cases was equal to 53.4 against 308 the average for the past three months, and 422 for the corresponding month last year. Thirty seven deaths occurred in Winwick Asylum, and exclusive of these the death rate proper for the district was 15S. The report was adopted.

LONGFORD DEPOT. NOTICE TO ABATE THE NUISANCE

Dr. SEPHTON reported that he visited Longford Depot along with the Inspector and found a very marked improvement both inside and outside the works. The nuisance from the chimney was very much less offensive than formerly, and the smell inside was considerably abated. He thought that when the reservoir was finished and a plentiful supply of water was obtained for con- densing the vapours, the nuisance so long complained of would be abated. Mr.iKNowLEs : When were yoti there last? Dr.SEPATON : A week last Wednesday. Mr. KNOWLES : • To-day the smell is about as bad as it can be. Dr. SEPHTON : The Inspector said he passed the works just before we went there, and there was not the slightest smell. On the Wednesday there was a very slight until but it was very faint. Mr. KNOWLES said that at one time there was not the slightest smell for three or four days when the wind was blowing towards the road. It shows they can prove• t the smell if they like. Dr.'SEPHTas : Of course they can. Mr. KNOWLES: A nuisance existed there this morn- ing. I propose that the nuisance inspector serves them with a notice to abate the nuisance from the pipe which conveys the vapours into Sankey Brook. Dr. SEPHTON : The nuisance can only be abated by condensing the vapours. Mr. KNOWLES pointed out that the condensation would cost the Corporation something for water. If they could not get enough "clean water' out of Sankey Brook they gave the thing up as a bad job. The Corporation water main passed close by. If a private person carriq€l on the same manufacture the Council would say "-What on earth has it to do with us. You must have a supply of water, and abate the nuisance." BURIED ALIVE.

A woman of Frieburg was buried on Saturday morning at Einsiedlin, the seat of the well-known shrine of the Black Madonna. The service at the grave was over,and the mourners had gone to Mass in the Abbey Church, when the gravedigger, who had began to fill in the grave, heard cries for help. The coffin was opened, and the woman was discovered to be still alive.

Sir,—Mr. Joseph Podmore, junr., President of the Trades and Labour Council, again denies my statement. No useful purpose, therefore, can be served by further continuing this controversy. Walking Day.

Sir,—Will you allow me, tlrough-the columns of your valuable paper to express to the kind friends in War- Tington who so generously contributed to the success of u r Walking Day celebrations, the grateful apprecia- tion and wdtmest thanks of all the workers and scholars of Emmanuel Church Sunday schools. Our collectors waited upon the townspeople as usual, and in view of the general depression of trade, it is most encouraging to know, that the list of contributors and the funds collected shows a considerable increase upon those of last year (though I do not yet know the exact figures of the latter) and were given with many expressions of hearty goodwill for our prosperity. We are deeply grateful, and at the conclusion of the day's romp with -the youngsters at Daresbury the ringing cheers from their young throats which had been lubricated with tplentifal supplies of new milk and cake, was the clearest 'possible evidence that these lambs of Christ's fold had greatly enjoyed their Walking Day celebrations of 1904, which will not soon be forgotten. In the name of the whole Church and Sunday school, I wish to thank all -our supporters for their good wishes,and the substantial help which has been forthcoming.—Yours faithfully, F. VAUGHAN (Curate-in-charge, Emmanuel Church.) 442 Knutsford-road.

The Social Problem.

Sir,—May I endeavour to reply as briefly as possible to the criticism of my letter by the Rev. H. Minnie°. In the first place, as a Socialist, I resent as most extreme, if not unjust, his assertion that "the party holds that it is capable of setting in order all things in heaven and earth." As no one has ever come from heaven to tell them of the state:of things there,they, in common with the rest of the human family, know nothing whatever about it, and have to say just as much as they know. They leave those matters to such as himself, and the church, who are paid to know more. And as to things on earth—well, they do but add their little iota to the process of evolution of the world's history, as do the Liberals and Conservatives, and with no greater pretentious than either. Mr. Dunnico goes on to say of the Socialists that "their adherents with few exceptions look on all other organisations as bitter rivals and upon those who hold aloof from their organ- isation with suspicion." This I strongly refute as not being correct. They receive all shades of opinion within their midst in the spirit of tolerance and brother- hood. One of their most honoured,if unwritten laws— and adhered to—is that if anyone comes in touch with them, or joins their organisation they shall be received in good-fellowship and ne questions shall be asked as to religious or political opinions. I notice that instead of keeping to the gist and point of my letter viz.,lthe impossibility of endeavouring to work the Liberal and Socialist cause at the same time, the reverend' gentleman defends himself by side-issues -under the cloak of his religion. I beg to point out that my letter was not on Christian Socialism versus non- Christian Socialism, nor upon religion at all, further than to use it as an analogy. Who has doubted Mr. I Dunnico's Christian bearing towards Socialism, or any- I thing else, or who has questioned his religious prin- ciples? I certainly did omit theword "Christian but then, I was writing of a minister of the Christian religion. I That being so, how was it possible for me to write of a Christian minister as being a non- Christian Socialist? I see no necessity for Mr. Dnnnico to qualify himself to be what every one else—myself included—took him to be as a matter of course. His qualification however (in that his Socialism is different to professing Socialism because Christian) suggests that the Socialists, as such, are not Christians. If so then Mr. Dunnico's Socialism is even a bit more narrow-minded than my own. There are as good Christians to be found amongst them, as amongst the Liberals and Tories—some of them clergy of various denominations. If there are non-Christians to be found amongst them there are also non-Christians amongst the Liberals and Tories. So why he "'should differentiate his, from ordinary Socialism because Christian I cannot conceive. If Mr. Dunnico, in his heart of hearts believes them to be non-Christian, if he thinks they treat others as bitter rivals, and look upon them with suspicion, then why does he place himself a Baptist minister, and Liberal enthusiast in such a false position as to champion the Socialist cause, advocate their principles by addressing their audiences and taking their chairs ?" But I do not think this his true attitude towards them and feel that his language occasionally gets the better of his more impartial judgment. His own friendly rela- tions towards them, and their's towards him are direct contradiction to his sarcastic criticism of them—at least of the Warrington branch. He asks will I "give a defini- tion of Socialism advocated by the party, and one which they will stand by." Well, pardon' no, not from dis- inclination nor perhaps inability but, lam but one of the least of their members, a mere unit amongst them and would not therefore presume to lay the law down for the guidance of men far superior to myself. If Mr. Dunnico really wishes such a definition may I recom- mend him to their intellect, their lecturers and chair- men. Men do not usually take the office of lecturer or chairman without at least knowing something of what they are going to talk about. I need not go into any argument re nationalisation of industry and property. If the principale of nationalisation is good for any pro- perty, any industry, then it is good for all, otherwise, where is the dividing line to be made, and who is to make it; the nation or the private owners of that which Should not be nationalised ? Which would try for the biggest pull I wonder! If the churches were all to be nationalised, and the ministry paid on the same principle as the Customs Post Office, etc., some pockets would greatly suffer thereby. Not that I think Mr. Dunnico would allow his principles to suffer for the sake of finance. I think that what hehonestly believes, that will he carry out to the best of his splendid ability. I wish him and the Social Service League, God-speed, though I cannot but feel that had my letter been written as a member of the Social Service League and on behalf of the Liberal cause, instead of that of Socialism it would not have been signalised as quite so illogical and narrow-minded by such an impartial and Christlike judgment as that of the Rev. H. Dunnico.—Yours sincerely, P. B. I

The Representation of Warrington. Sir,—ln your issue of last Saturday there is contained a letter from one "lona." I am not in a position to say what interest our present member •takes in the working classes; that can be hest answered by the working man himself, who, no donbt,is thoroughly con- versant with the attitude which he has adopted in Par- liament towards the Trades Unionist. But I would remind your correspondent that it is not in the interest of the British workman, or the commercial life of this country, to set class against class, and to inculcate into the workingman's mind that his biggest enemy is his employer. I think our friend will find that in Warring- ton, at least, the bulk of the intelligent working class element know that they have got a man in Mr. A. H. Crosfield who is broad-minded enough to realise that whatever is done to improve the condition of labour is appreciated by those participating in it. For example we have only to visit the works of which he is a director, to find that every encouragement is held out to those of its workpeople who have the interest of the firm at heart. Striking reforms have been inaugurated by the directors with a view of improving the social and physical conditions of its workpeople,reforms which must have called for considerable sacrifice on the part of the shareholder and directorate, both of a personal and financial character, which goes to emphasise the deep• and personal interest that Mr. Crosfield takes in the working classes, an interest which is not confined to those immediately in his employ, but has been given ex- nression to on the platform, time after time. It may be of interest to "lonat" and the employers,and working classes of Warrington, if I give a few of the improve- ments which Mr. Crosfield has helped to put in opera- tion at the works. Every employee who has been working for the firm continuously for 15 months prior to the first of April each year is allowed a week's holiday in the summer months with full pay. The choice of the date is left as much as is practicable to the individual. A paint is made of not refusing leave to any man during working time if sufficient reason is given for absence asked for. A Volunteer Company has been organized in the works which is attached to the Volunteer Battalion at Warrington. I believe they are the only firm in England who have so far taken this step. The managers are also encouraged to become Volunteer officers. Each volunteer is allowed to go a fortnight to camp every year, the firm paying full wages for one week and the difference between the amount of wages and the regimental pay for the second week. The men in the company are also allowed to put in an hour's drill per week in their working time. In order to further strengthen method, order and discipline, there is organized a strong fire brigade throughout the works, the men of which have to undergo military drill. Every six months, competi- tions between the men take place, the directors awarding prizes during the year to the amount of £3O. The firm are preparing the men to enter outside corn-

petitions with a view of eventually becoming possessors of the National Shield. The manning of the fire brigade is arranged with a view of gradually letting every young man in the firm's employment serve in the brigade. Physical development is promoted by encouraging games in the open air. There is an athletic club with sub-departments, consisting of football club, cricket club, and bowling club, also athletic sports. The clubs are all managed by committees elected by the men. They are self-supporting, the firm, how- ever, supplying the cricket field and certain outfit such as jerseys. The two cricket clubs and bowling clubs have also been very successful in outside com- petitions. Athletic sports are arranged annually, the firm supplying all the prizes. There is also a brass band (26 members) which is supported by the firm and managers, and which within the few years of its existence has worked itself up to be one of the best bands in the neighbourhood. It has already won a number of prizes in open competi- tions. The firm pay for the professional bandmaster to train the men, making a good attendance the oily condition, for the permanent continuance of this arrangement. A male and mixed choir have also been formed. These are also being trained to compete with outside choirs, the girls being trained,to sing 'part songs, and certain time every day will be set apart during the working hours for singing performances. The firm are now having plans prepared for dining rooms for both men and girls with baths and dressing rooms, also a library. The school and chapel which the firm owned specially for their own employees are beinp rebuilt, the members of the Crosfield family bearing the main burden of the expense. The works possesses a sick club,which is also self- supporting. The firm have given medical help and medicine free for many years. The men have now chosen their own doctor. The firm supply the con- sulting physician whenever required. The firm liberally subscribe to various infirmaries and hospitals both in Warrington and Manchester, in order to be able to take full advantage of them for the benefit of their employees. Special attentionhas bee-i paid by the firm to mental development of the employees. An investigation a few years ago showed that only a very small percent- age of the lads employed made use of the opportunities offered in the town for their educational benefit by evening classes. Statistics were taken and it was found that 13 per cent, of lads between 14 and 18 only attended technical schools. In order to increase the attendance the firm offered to pay the fees of any lessons taken by their employbs and also offered prizes at least equal in amdunt to the total fees to those who could prove a certain num- ber of attendances and whose master's report was satisfactory. The attendances rose from 13 per cent. to 50 per cent. the next year and 83 per cent. last year notwithstanding that the firm had increased the age limit from 17 to 20 years. Having achieved so large a percentage by the means just stated it was decided to make the attendances compulsory in future for lads in the employ of the firm under 17. This 4limit the firm hopes to increase eventually to 20 so that lads who enter their employ at 14 will have six years 'of compulsory schooling. In order to make the compulsion less irksome the firm continued their policy of paying all fees and offering substantial prizes. The firm has arranged with the manager of the educational department at Warrington for special courses. It has been pleasing to note that the firm's ex- ample in this respect is being either entirely or partly followed by several other influential firms in the neighbourhood. With regard to the girls, the directors are desirous of removing the odium which is so often attached to girls who work in factories. One of the 'conditions of employment in the works for the girls will be that at least two nights per week will have to be put to Technical instruction the principal subjects being needlework, dressmaking, laundry, cooking and domestic economy. Employing as the firm do several hundred girls it is hoped by this means in the course of some years to attain a very great improvement in the houses and homes of the workmen generally in this town. Each girl who leaves the works in order to get married receives from the firm a substantial wedding present. For general instruction the firm are supplying courses of lectures from time to time on subjects of importance. Fon example, a course of lectures on electricity was given just lately by thehead Professor, of Electricity from the Manchester Technical College for all employs specially interested in this subject. Lectures,concerts, smoking concerts, and social even- I ings are being arranged for the winter. The firm have arranged for the employs to elect al Workmen's Council on which the directors are not represented. The object of the Council is to collect suggestions from the workmen direct and to put these before the directors for approval. The suggestions are not in any way connected with the work but only with the welfare and the amelioration of the condition of labour generally in the works. All managers are instructed by the managing direc- tor to collect suggestions with regard to improve- ments connected with the works, and at the end of the year every manager sends in lists of the improve- ments made in his department, giving the names of those principally responsible for such improvements. To these employis,di rectors award substantial bonuses. The firm possesses an ambulance brigade, the centre of which is the laboratory, where a complete outfit of surgical instruments, bandages, etc., is kept. All lads and men are encouraged to attend ambulance lectures for which special arrangements are made every year, and prizes are given to those who satisfactorily pass examinations. A Challenge Shield has been given to be competed for by all corps in No. 4 district of the St. John Ambu- lance Brigade. I understand from the Deputy Com- missioner,Colonel Trimble,that this shield has stimu- lated, in no small measure the interest in ambulance work in the North of England. It has been the firm's object to reduce the hours of labour to a minimum and they have succeeded in most departments to do away with very early and very late hours. In most departments the men's time has been reduced from 10 hours to 9 hours a day,the total work- ing hours of 551 hours per week to 491, in some cases down to 46.1 hours, without alteration of wages. The girls' working hours have also been reduced, in this case from 551 to 461 throughout, without any reduc- tion. The firm has been successful in their system of profit-sharing, which they have introduced in several departments and intend to adopt this generally as far as possible. I maintain that these facts alone are sufficient proof of Mr. Crosfield's interest in the working classes, and it would suggest itself to me that the intimate knowledge which he displays of their wants is the best qualification we can have in a candidate for Parliamentary honours to represent a town like Warrington. While I take a deep interest—as others do—in the Labour movement in this town, I don't think we can have a better man at this juncture to champion its cause than Mr. Crosfield. In conclusion, if " lona " would only disclose his identity it would perhaps add greater weight to his correspondence. A champion Should not be ashamed co" his cause.—Yours truly,

W. FURNESS. Fern Lea, Great Sankey, NrWarrington,4th Ju1y,1904.

(ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.;

SOCIAL SERMONETTES FOR WORKING MEN.

BY JOHN TAYLOR. Author of "Search Lights on Land, Labour and Social Problems."

FREEMEN'S ALLOTMENTS AT LEICESTER.

"The Freemen of Leicester own more than one hundred and fifty acres of valuable land near the centre of the town. They have twenty-four cottages for aged Freemen and their widows; and their invested funds amount to more than eighteen thousand

pounds."—Report of Deputies for 1903: A stranger who enters Leicester for the first time by road or railway will wonder why there are so many hundreds and thousands of cottage gardens and small allotments on the outskirts of the town. If he asks how they came there, and who is the generous land- owner who provides them, he will be told that they belong ton Corporation of Freemen,who own the estate, and manage it on thoroughlydemocratic and progressive lines for their mutual benefit.

_ If he wants to know how this interesting property was first acquired and how it assumed its present form, and who has the control of it, he will probably get a delicate hint to mind his own business and ask no ques- tions. When I first began to unearth the history and mystery.of this social movement I found that nine out of ten of the freeholders knew nothing at all about these things, and the other tenth would not tell what they knew. Those whr% are "in the know" regard every inquisitive stranger with suspicion and distrust, so I had to unearth the history of Leicester for myself, and ransack all sorts of rare and curious documents till I got the information I wanted. T.—THE FREEMEN'S ALLOTMENTS HAD THEIR ORIGIN IN ANCIENT RIGHTS OF' FREE PASTURAGE.

Five or six hundred years ago the Merchants' Guilds i were a mighty power n the land, and the tradesmen and merchants of those days cheerfully took the oath of allegiance, and paid their !subscriptions and sought elections to these ancient trade unions, for the sake of the rights and privileges they conferred on their members. A guild merchant could become a master tradesman and employ journeymen and apprentices, and sell his own goods freely in the market. He was eligible for any public office, and he could vote for the Mayor and Cor- poration at municipal elections. But the possession of land .was the best key to politi- cal power. If a man owned fields, and meadows, and houses, he was a freeman, and could vote for a member of Parliament. So the highest ambition of the mer- chants' guilds was to acquire some landed property that would enfranchise every member of the fraternity. They were not allowed to buyland in those days, without the King's permission, but they might accept it as a gift, so many public-spirited members remembered them in their wills, and left them some real property to enfranchise them and make them all into freemen. So it came to pass in most towns that the merchants' guilds were strong enough to control the Corporation and dominate thie whole of its municipal life. But town councils and city corporations could not buy or sell, or hold any land, without the consent of the King, and most of our sovereigns were so, opposed to the growth of municipal authority that they refused their consent to this privilege. When they did give their permission the Corporation either had to pay a big bribe for it or they must get it by strategy and guile. In the thirteenth century the Corporation of Leicester was sorely in need of some land for municipal purposes and the King would not allow it to buy the land it re- quired, so Simon /le Montfort, the famous Earl of Leicester and the" Father of Parliaments," offered the citizens some land on a perpetual lease. In the words of an old chronicler," he gave them the exclusive priv- ilege of renting the South Fields for ever, at a nominal sum, on condition that the poor freemen should have the exclusive right of free pasturage on those fields, from October to March every year." By this arrangement the Corporation obtained con- trol of the South Fields Estate, subject to the rights of the poor freemen to the winter pasturage. It was a dual ownership that was certain to lead to quarrels and disputes some day, but it was from that tiny acorn of Common Rights that the mighty oak of Freemen's Allotments has grown. Now I have given you the genesis of this movement and shown you how the members of the Merchants' Guild became freemen and acquired common rights of pasturage over the South Fields. But these rights could only be retained and preserved by constant "usage." If the commoners could be bribed, or per- suaded or cajoled,or prevented from feeding their cattle they would lose their common rights altogether, and they could not renew them when once they had lapsed. lI.—THE CORPORA NON OF LEICESTER MADE MANY ATTEMPTS TO TAKE AWAY THE RIGHTS OF PASTURAGE.

But the commoners valued their privileges, and fought for them against all comers. The town began to grow, and the land rapidly increased in value. About the middle of the eighteenth century a perfect epidemic of land-grabbing set in, and the Mayor and Corporation made a determined effort to get rid of the claims of the freemen, And deal with the land as they pleased. \ They sold small portions of it to private individuals, but the purchasers found the freemen's cattle pasturing there as usual. If they drove them off they were served with writs and involved in lawsuits, and the freemen invariably won. Then the Corporation claimed the right "to exclude such freemen as they thought proper from turning any horses, or cows, or other cattle into the South Fields." This claim was to enable them to get rid of all the agitators and leaders who were fighting the freemen's battles and leave only timid, weak, old people on the list, who would submit to anything. But the commoners regarded this as a serious attempt to encroach on their ancient rights and privileges, and they kicked up such a lively row aboutit that the claim had to be abandoned.

Finally, the Corporation resolved to enclose all the fields and sell portions of the estate as building Sites. So they put up posts, and rails, and fences, and gates, and barriers of all sorts to exclude the freemen's cattle. This so enraged the commoners that in September, 1755 they marched in a body to South Fields and took forcible possession of the whole show to assert their rights. They spent a whole fortnight pulling down all the fences and barriers and holding a big bonfire there every night. Just to relieve the monotony of these proceedings,they made frequent incursions into the town to break the heads of the aldermen and town councillors who had voted for this land-grabbing. But these gentlemen got to know that they were to be interviewed, and they had some idea of the treat in!store for them if they fell into the hands of that mob; so they went away and left no address. So the angry freemen had to be content with smashing their windows and wreaking their shops and warehouses and promising them a handsome licking some other day. Then there were writs and lawsuits in abundance, but no judge or jury could set aside the ancient rights of the commoners to free pasturage on South Fields. The land-grabbers got the worst of it at every round, and they had to swear and pay. Finally the dispute was carried to Parliament, and it was settled by a very sensible compromise. The commoners agreed to accept a large plot of free- hold land as compensation for the loss of their grazing rights. HT.—THIS LAND WAS TO BE HELD AND MANAGED BY THE FREEMEN'S REPRESEN-

TAMES. Twenty-one desputies were elected to form the Board of Management, and seven of these retire from office each year, but they are eligible for re-election, so it is possible to change the entire board once in three years. The estate was originally laid out for the benefit of working men who desired to increase their income by gardening. The rules provided that each occupier must use only spade industry, and he must put one ton of good rotted manure every year on each twohundred and fifty yards of land. He must not encroach on his neigh- bours' land or leave any dirt or obstruction in the road and he must fence his plot at his own expence. He must pay a rent of one farthing per yard yelrly, or about half a guinea for a plot of five hundred square yards. The twenty-four cottages are to be let rent free, and when- ever any vacancy occurs the oldest freeman shall have the offer of it. But times have changed sinceithese rules were,framed. One or two railways have been made through the estate, and slices of land have been sold at fabulous prices. The proceeds of these sales have been invested in other plots of land in the town, orinConsols, or Metropolitan Stock. So the land is constantly increasing in value, and the Freemen's Corporation is growing so rich that it does not know what to do with its money. Rumour says that there are about fifteen hundred free- men on the books at the present time, and their free- hold land. and cottages, and investments are worth at least £150,000. It is impossible to verify these figures. because the officials refuse to give any information to outsiders. Very likely they may contradict my state- ment for diplomatic reasons, but they are not far from the mark after all. • The time has come when Parliament should overhau this wealthy Corporation and enlarge the powers of thl ileputie.i. They should build no more cottages, :but spend their profits in giving old-age pensions to every member over sixty-five years of age. Their last Act of Parliament was obtained in 1845, and it is high time that a new Act was secured to meet the altered circum- stances of the times. I visited ever so many of the allotments and talked with the freemen, and interviewed some of the deputies and I was delighted with all I saw and heard. The history of this movement helps us to realise what might have happened in every ancient town and city in England, if our forefathers had only shown the foresight, and courage, and dtgtermination of the men of Leicester. In most places the common rights were lost because the commoners did not "use" them, and insist onhaving them, and fight for them, if necessary. So the common lands were enclosed and captured by the manorial lords, and the parsons, and the greedy land-grabbers. Every

THE SCARLET FEVER EPIDEMIC.

EDUCATION COMMITTEE'S ACTION CRITICISED.

Discussion at the Town Council Meeting.

The outbreak of scarlet fever in the Borough, and the attitude of the Education Committee in declining to accede to the suggestion of the Medical Officer of Health that the schools should be closed immediately with the view of preventing the spread of infectious disease, were under discussion at the meeting of the Town Council• on Tuesday evening.

Alderman SUTTON in moving. the— confirmation of the minutes of the Health Committee was proceeding to refer to the outbreak of scarlet fever and the discus- sion upon it which took place at the last meeting of the Education Committee, when The 111A.voli said : This is not on the minutes. Alderman PIERPOINT : Let us hear him. (Hear hear.) The Council agreed, and Alderman Strrrox, continuing, said:—lt was clearly evident to me on reading the report of the Education Committee meeting that some of the speakers were altogether at sea and labouring under mistaken and erroneous ideas, both as regarded the action of Dr. Gornall and thevievvs of the Health Committee. Some of the statements they made were entirely uncalled for and unnecessary, reflecting as they did on the com- petency or ability of Dr. Gornall to advise on the ques- tion, and charging the Health Committee with a desire to shirk the responsibility of ordering the schools to be closed and to throw the onus of such a proceeding on the Committee of Education. Now I feel sure if those members of the Education ComMittee had taken the trouble to reflect for one moment, and had taken into consideration all that the Health Committee and Dr. Gornall ltd done during the last few years to prevent the spread of infectious diseases, and the time we had devoted in the work of providing new hospitals and in carrying Out the many important improvements which have been made in every department, they would have felt and understood the serious injustice of attempt- ing to publicly accuse either the committee or Dr. Gornall of the weak desire to throw any responsibility attached to their own duties on the shoulders of anyone else. (Hear, hear.) The Health Committee, I venture to say, is composed of members of the Corporation of a different stamp, who are quite capable of honestly transacting their own business, and fearless enough to bear the penalty for doing wrong. It has been fully I manifest to everybody that scarlet fever has been very prevalent since last November. Over 90 per cent. of the cases officially reported since then have been removed to the hospital, and I have it from Dr. Gornall that the brothers and sisters of the patients have in every case been quarantined to the extent that they have been excluded from school fora fortnight from the beginning of the attack in the house, and that this period of exclusion has been extended where second cases have occurred. The disease has not been confined to one part of the town or to any particular school, but has been very evenly distributed throughout the town. As the disease continued to increase, and the summer holidays were approaching, it was inquired from him by the Health Committee on the 27th of May last whether he considered it was advisable to begin the summer holidays earlier than usual as a means of decreasing the disease. His reply was that as no one school was very seriously affected he did not feel there were sufficient grounds for urging the committee to close any particular school, but that the question of the benefit likely to arise from a general closure was worth consideration. With the view, therefore, to ascertain the opinion of the Director of Education it appears that the doctor wrote to him in the first place explaining that the question had been mooted by the Health Committee, and expressing hiS own personal opinion that the immediate closing of all the schools for the summer holidays until the usual time fixed for the return of thel scholars would do a considerable amount of good. Dr.l Gornall not getting a reply to that communication (as the Director would no doubt naturally wish to have the instructions of his committee before replying) afterwards wrote to the Rev. Morley Stevenson, the chairman of the School Management Sub-committee, I giving his reasons why he thought the closing of the schools would be of advantage, which were something to the following effect :—" (1) Because such a prevalence of scarlet fever at this time of the year was unusual and if it increased as it usually did in the autumn at the rate it had been increasing it would be very ,bad indeed. (2) That as scarlet fever always diminished during the holidays, the schools could be more thoroughly disinfected during the holidays." Mr. Stevenson, in answer to that letter, did not express his own personal convictions, but stated that the Education Committee thought the Health Committee ought to have ordered the closure of the schools. The doctor would, however, he said, get an official reply, which reply subsequently came from the Director to the effect that the Education Committee were not satisfied that the closure would have the effect the doctor believed it would have, and that various School Board medical officers had given expression to different opinions on the subject. Now,frorn the parti- culars I think it will be evident to every member of this Council that in the action taken by Dr. Gornall his only object was to do what he considered best in the interests of the town. (Hear, hear.) Several members of the Health Committee along with myself as chair- man, being somewhat doubtful whether the closing of schools would diminish the epidemic do not desire to exercise our powers in any arbitrary manner, but would prefer to consult with the school authority in order to obtain its opinion on the question. Dr. GOrnall's inten- tion in seeking to ascertain that opinion, therefore, was to satisfy the committee before finally giving his decision It should not be forgotton, as the Committee were re- minded, that Dr. Gornall is medical officer to the Edu- cation Committee as well as to the Health Committee, and it might be considered discourteous on his part if he did not under such circumstances consult both com- mittees before taking any decisive measures. If, how- ever, the Committee of Education cannot condescend to consult with the Health Committee or their officers on so important a subject they must be satisfied to abide by the decision which may be given without their aid. With reference to the accusation made against Dr. Gornall for dismissing hospital patients at the most in- fectious stage of the disease and before they had properly recovered, the doctor deals with this matter in his report, but I am glad that Councillor Tinnion and Alderman Pierpoint denied the correctness of that assertion, and I was surprised that the gentleman who made it (who lam sure would not wilfully do an in- justice to anybody) should have in this case given credence to hearsay evidence and said what was cal- culated to injure a most painstaking official, whom it is our duty to protect so long as he performs his duties satisfactorily. (Hear, hear, and applause.) Councillor,EvANS seconded.

Alderman BENNETT said that although he was a mem- ber of the Education Committee he thought that in a matter of this kind which vitally affected the health of the town they ought to be guided by their Medical Officer-

The MAYOR (interrupting) : The Education Com- mittee has nothing to do with this. It is the Medical Officer's report we are discussing. Alderman BENrNETT said the matter wag important, as it vitally concerned the health of the town. They ought to be guided by the advice of their medical officer who was the responsible official and who was presumably thoroughly competent. If the health of the town was not satisfactory it was he who was criticised, and it was, therefore, illogical to hold a men responsible and not give him power to act. He was glad to hear Alder- man Sutton say that in any further emergency the Health Committee, if they found the courtesy was not reciprocated, would take the law into their own hands. The Health Committee was responsible for the health of the town. The health of the town was becoming a byword. For the third or fourth time in the last few months the death rate had been the highest in the kingdom. He could not sit still and see that state of things without expressing his humble opinion— The MA.Yort : You are trespassing You are talking about the Education Committee. Never mind the Education Committee—what they do or don't do. Alderman BENNETT, continuing, said he thought they were indebted to Dr. Gornall for the lucid report he had prepared. There was some element of doubt as to the best method of dealing with an outbreak of this character., He was supposed to know more about the matter than any member of the Council. They had not a doctor on the Council and he sometimes thought they ought to have one; but Dr. Gornall was supposed to have the necessary knowledge, and he was prepared to support him. (Hear, hear). Councillor PEN/BERTON Ras contending that any difference between committees ought to be brought be- fore the Council when

The MAYOR said: You must stick to the report of the Medical Officer. We cannot have discussions on the Education Committee. Councillor PrstnEnTos : There must be some mis- take.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Cricket.

All who are interested in the welfare of the Warring- ton Cricket Club will be sorry to learn that Lynes, who has been the professional for the past three seasons,will sever his connection with the eleven at the end of she present season. On Tuesday he signed on for Bicup, a club that at one time held a high position in the Lancashire League but which is at present at the bottom. It seems a pity that the committee of the Warrington club did not take such steps as would secure Lynes' services for another season. To him, undoubtedly, much of the credit is due for Warrington's unbeaten record this season, and if the proposed league in South-West Lancashire became an actual fact,Lynes' assistance would have been invalu- able.Bacup have offered him very generous terms,and he cannot be nlamed for looking at the matter from a monetary standpoint. Prior to coming to Warrington, he was pressed to go to Bacup, but preferred Warring- ton. The Bacup charmer has now succeeded, and his many friends here will watch his future career with interest.

Few cricket clubs can show such a good record this season as the Warrington club. On Saturday they gained another characteristic victory at the expense of Levenshulme, and thus maintained their unbeaten record. Levenhulme were all dismissed for 89 runs, and War- rington on going to the wickets ran up a total of 184. Lynes, fresh from the match at the Oval, again distin- guished himself with both bat and ball. He secured 5 wickets at an expense of 24 runs and carried out his bat for a well played 69. He hit freely all round the wicket and delighted the large gathering of admirers who lined the enclosure.

Levenshulme gave rather a tame display with the bat. The only player who batted with any confidence was Simister, who came out top scorer with 22. It was not until near the close of the innings that Podmore was given an opportunity of showing his skill with the ball, and his slow deliveries so troubled the visitors that he secured the last two wickets at an expense of only one run.

The initial batsmen on the Warrington side were P. B.Hawtin and W. H. Podmore,and runs coming quickly, 33 runs were scored before a separation took place. Podmore was the first to go, and he was caught behind the wickets through touching a rising ball from Simis- ter. Wright and Bibby followed in quick succession, and thre3 wickets were down for 35. Übsdell joined Hawtin, and a useful stand was made. Hawtin got in some good leg hits, and Übsdell liftbd the balls sent down by Richards with such vigour as to cause that bowler's retirement in favour of Norton. A good catch by Simister brought the partnership to a close, Übsdell being the retiring batsman. His score of 21 was a very creditable display, and came at a time when things were not looking very rosy for the home team. The bowling had been practically collared when Lynes came in,but he hit about him with such refreshing vigour that he soon overhauled the score of his partner,Hawti ?, who had been playing a patient and waiting game from the onset. The tevenshulme score was speedily passed and it was not until 126 had been scored that Hawtin's long innings concluded, he being taken at the wicket by Ingham. Lynes continued to score rapidly and at the close of the innings he was not out with a score of 69. His innings was one of the best witnessed on the Arpley enclosure, and was but 2 short of his score on the previous day at the Oval when playing for Lancashire second against Surrey second.

The second eleven visited Levenshulme,and the match ended in a draw. Warrington gave a pretty good account of themselves, thanks mainly to the L-rilliant batting of L. R. Connor who ran up 48. J. Thompson (22), A Thewlis (20), and W. Hind (15 not out) also did good work with the bat. Ths Wa.rrington innings real- ized 1511, and Levenshalme had compiled 113 for 8 when the time limit, was reached.

Crosfleld's gained an easy victory over Stretton and Appleton, J. Dargan, who made a capital 23, being their-top scorer. Not one of the villagers attained double figures.

Sankey Wire Mills gave a good account of themselves at Knotty Ash, and returned victorious by 39. F.Taylor was in fine form at the wicket awl knocked up 30. F'. Burton scored 16, and J. Hawthorn 11. Barley (19) gave the best display for the home team.

Latchford Baptists severely trounced Edge Green who made a miserable show. W. Southere did great things with the bat, his efforts being productive of 59.

After an exciting finish Walker's Brewery triumphed over Hough Green by the narrow. margin of 8. W. Ashton was responsible for a splendid 31 for the victors and W. Norarm compiled 15.

Warrington Co-operative entertained Birkenhead Co-operative on Tuesday, and proved victorious, their most successful batsmen being Bennion and AiLann who added 25 and 17 respectively. Banner jun., bowled effectively, capturing 7 wickets for 10 runs.

Bowling.

The holiday spirit permeated the local bowling fraternity on "Saturday, and there were no contests in connection with the Warrington eague or Works Association. To-day the semi-final of the Works Com- petition, which is arousing tremendous interest, will be decided on the King and Queen Green. The following teams will try conclusions : Houghton's Wire Works v. Cheshire Lines; referee, Mr. George Ball. Pearson and Knowles No. 1 v. Rylands No. 1 ; referee Mr. Tom Ellison.

Each match should be a very keen affair, but the chances of Pearson and Knowles' and Houghton's are particularly bright. In fact the forgemen will, I think, make a bold bid for the cup.

Next Saturday the final will take place, and a record gate is anticipated. The proceeds will be divided between two very deserving institutions—the Infirmary and District Nursing Association. The President (Captain G. R. Crosfield) is being invited to present the cup and medals after the match. On the following Saturday the cup winners will meet a team selected from the various teams which have figured in the com- petition, and the proceeds on this occasion will also go to the Infirmary and Nursing Association.

The Warrington and District League matches to-day will be : Fearnhead v. Horse and Jockey. Brooklands v. Chapel House. Penketh Park v. Bull's Head. On Thursday Fearnhead will meet Chapel House.

At a League Committee meeting on Wednesday evening it was decided to present a gold medal to the player who achieves the best individual performance throughout the season, and twelve gold centre medals to the runners up. Mr. A. il. Crosfteld has promised to present a cup and medals to the team which heads the league, and the Committee will give gold-centre medals to the runners-up.

The contest for the cup presented by Mr. Allison t the Cemetery Club took place on Wednesday evening in the presence of a large gathering. In the semi-final, Antrobus defeated Boardman by 21-17 and the final resulted in a victory for Antrobus by 21-'l3. In addition to holding the cup the winner receives a gold medal, and the other prize-winners are Kay, Blardman, and Vernon.

Cycling. As most of the NVarrington Cycling Club were away last week end, Saturday's run to Chester was cancelled. Several members had a tour through Derbyshire and Nottinghamshire, returning by Derbyshire and Cheshire. and from all accounts they appear to have had a good time. Another section of the club made Blackpool its headquarters for the week-end, and a third contingent again took the opportunity of visiting the lovely Vale of Llangollen. The.outings were a great success.

MOTOR NOTES.

In a recently reported ?ase in which a clergyman was charged with driving his car furiously, the evidence showed that he did not slow up sufficiently upon encountering a flock of sheep, with the result that five of them were injured, one so badly that it had to be immediately destroyed whilst a second died the follow- ing day. A witness, called by the defendant, stated that the driver had done his utmost to stop the car directly he saw the sheep in the t oad. Then asked the deputy- Chief Constable in all solemnity, "Would not the proper way to pass a flock like that be to bring the car to a standstill?" This almost equals the piece of advice earnestly tendered to cyclists years ago by one who is now a high authority in the Cyclist's Touring Club; "In overtaking a cow, always keep behind her."

When the motorist suddenly encounters sheep, it is undoubtedly the best plan to stop the car and let the flock pass by,or else to proceed at quite a walking pace. Unless there is a good sheep-dog in charge, it is hope- less to expect that the shepherd will be of much use in keeping the road clear by marshalling his flock upon one side, and a good deal of future trouble may ba avoid by keeping the car stationary for a few minutes.

In the case just referred to, the police did not charge the driver with exceeding the legal limits, but, very properly under the circumstances took out the summons against him under the first section of the new act. In- deed his pace was, according to the constable, about 14 miles an hour. We have all known instances where ex- cessive speed has been sworn to, even though we have had excellent reasons, connected with the mechanism of the car, to know that such has just then been impossible but in this case, taking the evidence into consideration it would seem that the police had made a really mod- erate estimate. That speed would not be at all fast, even for taking the slight curve which occurred just before the sheep were encountered,and the car ought to have been under such control that it could have been pulled up instantly.

The Chairman of the Benclit in giving his decision,de- clared that, as a rule, motorists gave them very little trouble,and added his personal opinion that it is not the pace sol much as the manner in which the cars are driven that causes danger to the public. This undoubt- edly is so, and if we had only had a few clear-headed men of this stamp to represent our interests last year, we should not have been hampei•ed with the present un- satisfactory Act. Even such a case as this could have, been stringently dealt with under the Highways Act. Thespeed limit was not required in the least. What was required was that motoring hooligans should be sum- moned for furious driving, or driving to the common danger, without any question as to whether their rate of travelling was ten or twenty miles an hour. There are motorists on the road to-day—not inany,one is thankful to add—who drive to the common danger at the former figure, and there are many others who would be per- fectly safe upon country roads with their cars doing forty.

A writer in the" Globe" insists that "the majority of live axles on the market are badly designed and except in the case of two or three makers, it is rare to see a car of this type with the rear wheels running true after a year's hard use." Unfortunately, this indict- ment is correct, so far as very many of the live axle cars are concerned. The reason undoubtedly lies in the fact that the average axle of ordinary design is not strong enough to support the weight that is thrust upon it and to take the driving strain as well. Therefore, it is not long before the axle sags and the strain and fric- tion on the differential increase.

Now, the transmission of power to the road-wheels is Undoubtedly best accomplished by the propeller shaft and live axle; but the drawbacks referred to are serious. To obviate these the Societe Decauville of Paris adopt the following plan. A hollow fixed axle supports the weight and on its ends the wheels take their bearings. Inside the axle are carried what are really two pro- peller shafts, and these are suitably connected with the differential and the hubs of the road-wheels. Conse- quently, the advantages of both types of axle are in this waysecured, and, as an illustration of the immense success of the device. the Motor Car Co, Ltd., who are the British agents for the Societe Decauville, show a 12 h.p. Decauville whose mileage now exceeds 24,000 miles and whose rear wheels run as truly as they did upon the first day that the car left the factory.

Two more attempts upon Vie nrotor-cycle'record from Land's End to John o' Groats are in contemplation for the immediate future. Indeed, it is more than plssible that they may have become actual facts before these lines are in print. It is stated that the Rev. B. H. Davies entertains strong hopes of beating the present time made by Mr. G. P. Mills.

Goggles are so very necessary for the motorist who does much fast or long-distance travelling that it is rather surprising they are so often purchased without due care being exercised concerning the pattern chosen. Those with silk continuation pieces are not nearly so satisfactory as is the type having these made of leather. The wind seems to gee between the silk and the wearer's skin, and in this manner a concentrated draught is con- veyed to the eyes, which is really more troublesome than if no protection at all were worn. The leather i flaps, on the other hand, keep well n place, and perfect immunity from wind and draught is thus secured. There is a slight difference in the price, but in matters of this kind that should be quite a secondary considera- tion, particularly as such difference is very small. The appealsof Earl Russell against a conviction by the Winchester magistrates for driving his car to the danger of the public has been dismissed with costs. It will be remembered that the evidence showed he had swung round a cornsr in a village at a speed estimated at twenty miles an hour, and, in pulling out to overtake a dog-cart, pearly ran a cyclist down who was riding The cyclist, who dismounted and safety on the bank, said that when the cstcoarrwaapmradbsslseed fordhilni. it was within two inches of one of his hands. whilst the measurements taken of the off-side wheel mark showed that there was only just a foot between that and the edge of the road. out, accompanied by several friends, and after a plea- sant run arrived at the "Swan." Several games of bowls were indulged in with great interest. After a brief stay for refreshments the party set out for home and an uneventful ride—with only one punctura—con- cluded an enjoyable outing. -- To-day the club make their annual visit to Beeston Tower where they will be the guests of Mr. John Naylor, the esteemed president. There is no more popular fixture than "President's Day" and given a continuance of the beautiful weather, there should be a grand muster. -- Tuesday evening's run will be to Glazebury. -- Great preparations are being made for the champion- ship races in connection with the Liverpool centre of the N.C.U. next Saturdayat New Brighton. The events will include the fifty miles and half-mile championships. The council of the Liverpool centre of the N.C.U. discussed an important proposal on Tuesday night. The Derby Centre has issued a circular drawing attention to the unsatisfactory condition of the finances of the union. Office expenses at London were £425 17s. 6d. during the year, and were constantly increasing. The circular pro- posed that it be decentralised and four districts formed —Leeds, Derby, Bristol, and London respectively, Mr. Pickavance (Warrington), Councillors A. A. W. Phillips (Southport), and Mr. J. G. Waldron (hon. secretary) spoke on the position. Mr. Pickavance proposed, and the meeting accepted a resolution thanking the Derby Centre for their circular, and stating that while not agreeing with everything embodied in the circular, the Liverpool Centre would heartily support any proposal brought forward for maintaining the efficiency of the union, with a reduction of the present heavy expendi- ture. -- - LIGHTING-UP TIME. To-night Sunday Monday 9.35 Tuesday 9.34 Wednesday Thursday Friday Water Polo. The Warrington teams played two matches at Everton on Thursday evening. The second contingent tried conclusions with Everton third in a third division con- test,, and suffered defeat by four goals to one. The visitors were poorly represented, and Everton proved much the superior team. Monks scored the goal for Warrington. The first team fared better in their en- counter with Everton second, and gained the victory by three goals to nil. In the first half, Warrington defended the deep end, and at half-time neither side had broken through the other's defence. On resuming, Warrington outplayed the home team, goals being registered by Warren, Allen and Boscow. The return matches will be played at Warrington on the 19th inst. The same evening he second round of the works squadron competition will be decided, the following teams meeting : Pearson and Knowles v. Fletcher's. Co-operative Stores v. Crosfield's. Rylands Bros. v. Richmond's. • Foot Racing. At the Wrexham Cycling Club sports, on Saturday, J. Hughes, Sankey Harriers (16), won the third prize in thd 228 yards flat race handifap. At the annual English championship meeting at Rock- dale C. J. Straw of Warringt )n A. C.was third lathe two miles steeplechase. S. J. Robinson, of Northampton, the holder did not run, and the first position fell to A. Russell, of Walsall, J. J. Daly, of Galway County,being second.

From this it would seem that the leviathans are really over-powered, and that 80 h.p. iS abont the limit which will prove practicable. By the way, it is difficult to see how England is !;o make the suggestion by which she proposes to reduce the great race to a reliability trial, pure and simple. She hse been hopelessly outclassed in a speed contest, and ,• although it may be argued that these proposals were afoot before the race was run in Germany,it will also be remembered that the most optimistic of those sch,° knew anything at all about the matter never supposed', in their wildest flights of fancy, that Germany and France were entertaining cars which would give us the faintest chance to win with those chosen to represent ourselves.

STOCKTON HEATH. [Continued from, page 6.] PARISH COUNCIL. Immediately on the close of the Parochial Committee meeting, the Parish Council met under the presidencY of Mr. Clarke. There were also present :—Messrs. J. Westbrook, T. R. Westbrook, Mclntyre, Williams, Whitley, Hallows, Mounfield, Welsby, Warburton, and ' E. Bradshaw (elerk.) THE WARRINGTON FIRE ENGINE. In reply to a letter from Mr. Bradshaw, the Town Clerk wrote stating that he was working out a scheme' of contributions from all the adjoining parishes to the expenses of the fire brigade department. LIABILITY REPUDIATED. The Clerk to the County Council wrote respecting 'damage recently done to a tree and guard in GrapPen- hall-road through a horse taking fright at a County Council steam roller. The matter, he stated,had bee" considered by the Main Roads and Bridges Committee, and in as much as the tree was planted by the Parish Council at their own risk, the committee could not recommend the County Council to comply with there- , quest of the Parish Council to make good the injury. SUGGESTED EVENING CLASSES. A letter was received from Mr. F. Ashton, stating that at a meeting of the Education Committee for the Runcorn Rural District he was instructed to ascertain from the local committees, school managers, and parish 'councils, what evening classes were suggested for the various parishes within the committee's district during' the coming session. He had written the managers of the day schools in the parish, and the Council might find it of advantage to discuss the question with theln• The Chairman said the letter was important,, and one of which they ought to take advan- tage if they could get sufficient students to attend the classes at Stockton Heath. Surely in a place of 3,000 or 4,000 inhabitants the necessary number of students should be forthcoming. He understood the cost of the classes would not be a special charge uP°l4' the village, but would be included in the county rate. Mr. Whitley said Mr. Rose had trie-1 n., laveral occa- sions to get classes together, but the siteme had nnt been a success. Mr. H. J. Wastbrook remarked that if the best classes, were in Warrington the young people would go there. It was decided that the Caairm tn, Alassrs. Westbrook. Mclntyre, and the Clerk should consult with the school managers on the matter. "A HOT BED OF PROFANITY." Mr. Hallows said the seats round the trees oppceit! the Mulberry Tree required renovating. If the seal's' near the Swing Bridge were not raised they would sooa have to be dug out of the ground. The Chairman said 'that when the trams came t° Victoria-square the seats there would have to be removed. At present they were simply places for people to loaf about. Mr. T. R. Westbrook said the seats were "a hotbed of profanity," breeding nothing but bad language. DELAYS AT THE SWING BRIDGE. Mr. Hallows complained strongly of the inconveni- ence to the public caused by only one man being employed to take charge of the Swing Bridge when it was opened and closed! Business people and working men were put to a great loss of time. When the bridg? had been closed, the people had to wait at one end until the man ran across the bridge to open the gate. Mr. Welsby thought the inconvenience would be' obviated when the tram service was introduced. The Clerk was instructed to write to the Ship Canal Company asking them to employ two men regularly ou the bridge. THE VOLUNTEER ENCAMPMENT.
